Strong Priority and Determinacy in Timed CCS
Luigi Liquori, Michael Mendler

Abstract
Building on the standard theory of process algebra with priorities, we identify a new scheduling mechanism, called constructive reduction which is designed to capture the essence of synchronous programming. The distinctive property of this evaluation strategy is to achieve determinacy-by-construction for multi-cast concurrent communication with shared memory. In the technical setting of CCS extended by clocks and priorities, we prove for a large class of coherent processes a confluence property for constructive reductions. We show that under some restrictions, called pivotability , coherence is preserved by the operators of prefix, summation, parallel composition, restriction and hiding. Since this permits memory and sharing, we are able to cover a strictly larger class of processes compared to those in Milner’s classical confluence theory for CCS without priorities.

Theory of computation, Process algebras

keywords
Timed process algebras, determinacy, priorities, synchronous programming

Strong Priority and Determinacy in Timed CCS

1
Introduction

Concurrency, by expression or by implementation, is both a convenient and unavoidable feature of modern software systems. However, this does not mean that we must necessarily give up the requirement of functional determinism which is crucial for maintaining predictability and to manage design complexity by simple mathematical models
[
23
,
8
,
13
]
.
While the pure
\lambda
-calculus is naturally deterministic by design, it cannot model shared memory. Process algebras can naturally model shared objects, but do not guarantee determinism out of the box.
In this paper we use the standard mathematical formalism
CCS

[
27
]
to study methods for reconciling concurrency and determinism.

Determinacy in Process Algebra

In
CCS
, the interaction of concurrent processes
P\,|\,Q
arises from the rendezvous synchronisation of an
action

\alpha
of
P
and an associated
co-action

\overline{\alpha}
from
Q
, generating a
silent
action
\tau
, also called a
reduction
.
A process is in
normal form
if cannot reduce any more and
determinate
if it reduces to at most one normal form, up to some notion of structural congruence.

As a simplified example for the scenarios that we are interested in, consider a triple of concurrent processes
R\,|\,S\,|\,W
, where
S\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}r{\mathsf{.}}S+w{\mathsf{.}}\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}

acts as a shared resource, such as a (write-once) store, while
R\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
,
W\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
are read and write processes, respectively, sharing
S
with each other. The store
S
offer a read action
r
(rendezvous) leaving the state unchanged, or a write action
w
upon which it changes its state to
\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
. At this point, the store only permits a single read and then becomes the inactive process
{{\mathsf{0}}}
.
The synchronisation between the actions
r
,
w
and co-actions
\overline{r}
,
\overline{w}
generates two sequences of reductions from
R\,|\,S\,|\,W
:

\centering{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\underset{}{\xleftarrow{\tau}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,S\,|\,W\underset{}{\xleftarrow{\tau}}R\,|\,S\,|\,W\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}R\,|\,\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}.\@add@centering

Obviously,
R\,|\,S\,|\,W
is not determinate. It reduces to two distinct normal forms
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}
. The final store
\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
on the left permits one more read, while
{{\mathsf{0}}}
on the right does not.

A sufficient condition for determinacy is determinism.
A process
P
is
(structurally) deterministic
if for all its derivatives
Q
and action
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
,
if
Q\xrightarrow{\alpha}{}{}Q_{1}
and
Q\xrightarrow{\alpha}{}{}Q_{2}
then
Q_{1}\equiv Q_{2}
, where
\equiv
is a suitable structural congruence.
However, determinism is too strong. For instance, the store
P\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}R_{1}\,|\,S\,|\,R_{2}
with two readers
R_{i}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}R_{i}^{\prime}
(
i{=}1,2
)
is determinate but not deterministic, when the
R_{i}
may
reach structurally distinct states
after reading, say,
P\xrightarrow{\tau}R_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,S\,|\,R_{2}
and
P\xrightarrow{\tau}R_{1}\,|\,S\,|\,R_{2}^{\prime}
where
R_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,S\,|\,R_{2}\not\equiv R_{1}\,|\,S\,|\,R_{2}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, determinism is not closed under parallel composition. In the above example, all process
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
,
S
are deterministic, but their composition
P
is not.
An insightful solution, proposed by Milner (Chap. 11 of
[
27
]
), is to replace determinism by the notion of
confluence
which still implies determinacy but turns out to be closed under parallel composition, under natural restrictions.

Definition 1
.

P
is (structurally) confluent
1
1

1

A variation of Milner’s “strong confluence” that includes
\tau
-actions and uses
\equiv
not bisimulation
\sim
.
if for every derivative
Q
of
P
and reductions
Q\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}\!\!{}\,Q_{1}
and
Q\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}\!\!{}\,Q_{2}
with

\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
or

Q_{1}\not\equiv Q_{2}
, there exist

Q_{1}^{\prime}\equiv Q_{2}^{\prime}
such that
Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}\!\!{}\,Q_{1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{2}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}\!\!{}\,Q_{2}^{\prime}
.

Milner shows that confluence is preserved by

“confluent composition”

P{\,|\,}_{\!L}Q\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}(P\,|\,Q)\,\backslash\,L
, combining the parallel and restriction operators, subject to the condition that
\mathcal{L}(P)\cap\mathcal{L}(Q)=\{\,\}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}(P)}\cap\mathcal{L}(Q)=L\cup\overline{L}
, where
\mathcal{L}(R)
is the
sort
of a process
R
.

This is a form of
sort separation
, ensuring that every action
\alpha
has at most one synchronisation partner
\overline{\alpha}
either inside or outside of
(P\,|\,Q)\,\backslash\,L
.
Many practical examples of determinate systems can be understood as
sort-separated compositions of confluent processes. However, this still
covers only a rather limited class of applications.
Most seriously, memory processes such as
S
above
are intrinsically not confluent, and even if they were, confluent composition
forbids direct multi-cast communication, because labels such as
r
in
S
could not be shared by two readers
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
due to sort-separation.
This means that concurrent programming languages that support shared memory and yet have
determinate reduction semantics, cannot be handled.

Determinacy in Synchronous Programming

The most prominent class of shared-memory programming languages that manage to reconcile concurrency and determinacy, is known as
Synchronous Programming
(SP). In SP, processes interact with each other asynchronously at a
micro-step
level, yet synchronise in lock-step to advance jointly in iterative cycles of synchronous
macro-steps
. The micro-step interactions taking place during a macro-step determine the final outcome of the macro-step. This outcome is defined at the point where all subsystem pause to wait for a global logical
clock
. When this clock arrives, all subsystem proceed into the next computation cycle.
Under the so-called
synchrony hypothesis
, the final outcome of a macro-step for each subsystem is fully determined from the stimulus provided by the environment of that component during the micro-step interactions.
As such, the interactions inside each subsystem at macro-step level can be abstracted into a global Mealy automaton with deterministic I/O.

SP started with Statecharts
[
17
]
and has generated languages such as Signal
[
14
]
, Lustre
[
16
]
, Esterel
[
5
]
, Quartz
[
37
]
, SCCharts
[
41
]
, just to mention a few.

When it comes to mathematical modelling it is natural to think of a clock as a broadcast action in the spirit of Hoare’s
CSP

[
19
]
that acts as a synchronisation barrier for a set of concurrent processes. Used with some scheduling control, it can bundle each process’ actions into a sequence of macro-steps that are aligned with each other in a lock-step fashion. During each macro-step, a process executes only a temporal slice of its total behaviour, at the end of which it waits for all other processes in the same
clock scope
to reach the end of the current phase. When all processes have reached the barrier, they are released into the next round of computation.
This suggests a priority-based scheduling mechanism, i.e., that the clock should fire only if the system has stabilised and no other admissible data actions are possible. This principle, called
maximal progress
, is built into timed process algebras, see e.g.
[
18
,
11
]
.

Contribution

We introduce an extension of
CCS
, denoted
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, that brings together the concepts of
priorities

[
10
,
36
,
33
]
and
clocks

[
15
,
11
,
32
]
that have previously been studied for
CCS
, but independently.
We thus obtain an adequate compositional setting for SP based on standard techniques from process algebra. This is surprising since many different customised semantics (e.g.,
[
24
,
9
,
5
,
1
]
) have been developed for SP over the years, few of which fit into the classical framework of
CCS
.
Similar ideas might be possible for other process algebras like
ATP

[
31
]
,
TPL

[
18
]
,
CSP
or some synchronous variants of Milner’s
\pi
-calculus
[
2
]
, just to mention a few. We find
CCS
most plausible as a point of departure since the concept of priorities is already well established and the combination of asynchronous scheduling and communication via local rendezvous synchronisation
makes the problem of ensuring determinacy more prominent.

Our calculus
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
provides a natural setting
to take a fresh look at confluence in
CCS
as an adequate mathematical model for SP. Moreover, we envisage that
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, extended by value-passing, can be used as a playground to study compositional embeddings of concurrent
\lambda
-calculi. In particular, we have in mind those with sharing, as required for the lazy semantics of Haskell and with deterministic memory such as IVars/TVars
[
25
]
and LVars
[
22
]
.
At the technical level, we make the following specific contributions in this paper:

\bullet
We use broadcast actions (clocks) as global synchronisation barriers to schedule processes using priorities in a more flexible way than any of the earlier approaches. While
PMC

[
15
]
has clocks but no priorities and in
CSA

[
11
]
the priorities are hardwired and expressing precedence only between the rendezvous actions and the clock, we use a fully general priority scheme as in Phillips’
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}

[
33
]
.
By adding clocks to
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
we generalise previous
CCS
extensions such as Milner’s
SCCS

[
26
]
,
PMC
or
CSA
for expressing synchronous interactions of concurrent processes. To achieve this, we must strengthen Phillips’ notion of
weak enabling
(Def.
2
) by
constructive enabling
(Def.
3
). While all classical extensions of
CCS
by priorities
[
10
,
36
,
33
]
schedule the processes by their immediate initial actions, constructive enabling takes into account all actions potentially executable up to the next clock barrier.

\bullet
We identify a strictly larger class of processes that enjoy the Church Rosser (CR) Property (and thus reduce to unique normal forms) than the “confluence class” discussed by Milner, see e.g., Chap. 11 of
[
27
]
. Since priorities are not part of the classical confluence theory, it cannot model deterministic shared objects with conflicting choice. In this paper, exploiting priorities and clocks, we enrich Milner’s notion of confluence to “confluence up-to priorities” that we call
coherence
(Def.
12
). We show that coherent processes are CR for constructive enabling (Thm.
14
).

\bullet
We show that coherence is preserved by parallel composition under reasonable restrictions that permit sharing and memory (Thm.
24
). Specifically, we construct coherent processes in a type-directed compositional fashion using the notion of
precedence policies
(Def.
16
). The policy for a conformant process specifies an upper bound for its precedence-blocking behaviour (Def.
17
). A policy enriches the classical sort of a process by priority information. We define the special class of
pivot policies
and call coherent processes conforming to them
pivotable processes
(Def.
C.51
). These are not only coherent (and thus CR) but preserved by parallel composition and other operators of the language (Thm.
24
). In this fashion, we are able to extend Milner’s classical results for confluent processes to cover significantly more applications, specifically Esterel-style multi-cast SP with shared objects.

\bullet

We show that each coherent process
is
clock-deterministic
(Prop.
15
) and that pivotable processes satisfy
maximal progress
(Prop.
23
).
No matter in which order we execute constructively enabled reductions, when the normal form is reached, and only then, a clock can tick. Since the normal form is uniquely determined, each pivotable process represents a “synchronous stream”. As such, our work extends the classical non-deterministic theory of timed (multi-clocked) process algebras
[
15
,
18
,
11
]
for applications in deterministic synchronous programming.

For lack of space, four separate appendices will present (A) how research towards determinacy for process algebras passes through those above cited papers, (B) a collection of more tricky examples, and (C,D) the full proofs.

2
A Process Algebra with Clocks and Priorities

The syntax and operational semantics of our process algebra ‘SynPaTick’, denoted
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, is defined in this section.

2.1
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
Syntax

The terms in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
form a set
\mathcal{P}
of
processes
. We use
P,Q,R,S\ldots
to range over
\mathcal{P}
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a set of
process names
and let
A,B\ldots
range over
\mathcal{I}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a countably infinite set of
channel names
with
a,b\ldots
ranging over
\mathcal{A}
. As in
CCS
, the set
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
is a disjoint set of
co-names
with elements denoted by
\overline{a},\overline{b},\overline{c}\ldots
in bijection with
\mathcal{A}
; The overbar operator
switches between
\mathcal{A}
and
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e.,
\overline{\overline{a}}=a
for all
\overline{a}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
.
We will refer to the names

a\in\mathcal{A}
as
input
(or
receiver
) labels while the co-names
\overline{a}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
denote
output
(or
sender
) labels.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a countably infinite set of broadcasted
clock names
, disjoint from both
\mathcal{A}
and
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
and let
\sigma
range over
\mathcal{C}
. Every clock acts as its own co-name,
\overline{\sigma}=\sigma
and so each
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}
is also considered as a
clock label
.
Let
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{A}\cup\overline{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathcal{C}
be the set of input, output and clock
labels
and let
\ell
range over
\mathcal{L}
, while
L,H
range over subsets of
\mathcal{L}
. We write
\overline{L}
for the set
\{\overline{\ell}\mid\ell\in L\}
. Let
\mathit{Act}=\mathcal{L}\cup\{\tau\}
be the set of all
actions
obtained by adjoining the
silent
action
\tau\notin\mathcal{L}
and let
\alpha
range over
\mathit{Act}
. Viewed as actions, the input and output labels
\ell\in\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\mathcal{A}\cup\overline{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathit{Act}
are referred to as
rendezvous actions

to distinguish them from the
clock actions

\sigma\in\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathit{Act}
.
All symbols can appear indexed.
The process terms
\mathcal{P}
are defined thus:

\begin{array}[]{lcll@{\quad}l}P,Q,R,S&::=&{{\mathsf{0}}}&&\text{stop (inaction)}\\
&\mid&A&&\text{name, }A\in\mathcal{I}\\
&\mid&\alpha{:}H.P&&\text{action, }\alpha\in\mathcal{L}\text{, }H\subseteq\mathcal{L}\\
&\mid&P+Q&&\text{choice}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lcll@{\quad}l}\\
&\mid&P\,|\,Q&&\text{parallel}\\
&\mid&P\,\backslash\,L&&\text{restriction}\\
&\mid&P\,/\penalty\,L&&\text{hiding}\end{array}

Intuitively,
{{\mathsf{0}}}
denotes the
inactive
process which stops all computations, including clock actions. The
action prefix

\alpha{:}H.P
denotes a process that offers to engage in the action
\alpha
and then behaves as
P
while the
blocking
set
H\subseteq\mathcal{L}
lists all actions taking precedence over
\alpha
. A prefix with
\alpha\in\mathcal{R}
denotes a
CCS
-style rendezvous action. In case
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
, it denotes a
CSP
-style broadcast action that can communicate with all the surrounding processes sharing on the same clock only when the clock is universally
consumed
.
We assume that in every restriction
P\,\backslash\,L
the set
L\subseteq\mathcal{R}
consists of rendezvous actions, and in every hiding
P\,/\penalty\,L
we have
L\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. These
scoping
operators act as name binders that introduce local scopes. The acute reader will notice the absence of the relabelling operator
P[f]
, useful in
CCS
to define the well-known “standard concurrent form”
(P_{1}[f_{1}]\,|\,\cdots\,|\,P_{2}[f_{n}])\,\backslash\,L
. Because all of our examples are captured without need of relabelling,
and for the sake of simplicity, relabelling will be treated in the full version of this work.
A useful abbreviation is to drop the blocking sets if they are empty or drop the continuation process if it is inactive. For instance, we will typically write
a{\mathsf{.}}P
instead of
a{:}\{\,\}{\mathsf{.}}P
and
a{:}H
rather than
a{:}H{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
. Also, it is useful to drop the braces for the blocking set if it is a singleton, writing
a{:}b{\mathsf{.}}P
instead of
a{:}\{b\}{\mathsf{.}}P
.

In our concrete notation using the abstract syntax of processes, we assume that the binary operator
+
is right associative and taking higher binding strength than the binary operator
\,|\,
, i.e., writing
P+Q+R\,|\,S
instead of
(P+(Q+R))\,|\,S
. Also, we assume that the unary operators of prefix, restriction and hiding have higher binding strength than the binary operators. Hence, we write
P\,\backslash\,c+a{\mathsf{.}}b{\mathsf{.}}c{\mathsf{.}}Q\,|\,R\,/\penalty\,\sigma
instead of
((P\,\backslash\,c)+(a{\mathsf{.}}(b{\mathsf{.}}(c{\mathsf{.}}Q))))\,|\,(R\,/\penalty\,\sigma)
.
The
sub-processes
of a
P
are all sub-expressions of
P
and (recursively) all sub-processes of
Q
for
definitions

A\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}Q
where
A
is a sub-expression of
P
.
Sometimes it is useful to consider fragments of
\mathcal{P}
. Specifically, a process is called
unclocked
if
P
does not contain any clock prefix
\sigma{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
as a sub-process. A process is called
sequential
or
single-threaded
if it does not contain any parallel sub-process
Q\,|\,R
.

P
is
closed
if
all sub-processes
\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
in
P
occur in the scope of a restriction or hiding operator that binds
\ell
.

As usual, we denote by
\mathcal{L}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{L}
the free labels, called
(syntactic) sort
of
P
,
identify processes that differ only in the choice of bound labels.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{5mm}}rcl@{\quad}l}({\sf Comm})\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&P\star Q&\equiv&Q\star P\\[2.84526pt]
({\sf Assoc})\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(P\star Q)\star R&\equiv&P\star(Q\star R)\\[2.84526pt]
({\sf Zero})\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&P\star{{\mathsf{0}}}&\equiv&P&\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}rcl@{\quad}l}({\sf Idem}_{+})&P+P&\equiv&P&\\[2.84526pt]
({\sf Scope}_{\wr})&P\wr L\wr L^{\prime}&\equiv&P\wr(L\cup L^{\prime})\\[2.84526pt]
({\sf Scope}_{{{\mathsf{0}}}})&{{\mathsf{0}}}\wr L&\equiv&{{\mathsf{0}}}&\end{array}

\noindent\hskip-5.69054pt\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}rcl@{\quad}l}({\sf Scope}_{\alpha})&(\alpha{:}H.P)\,\backslash\,L&\equiv&\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\mathsf{0}}}\\
\alpha{:}H.(P\,\backslash\,L)\end{array}\right.&\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{if }\alpha\in L\cup\overline{L}\\
\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\\[2.84526pt]
({\sf Scope}_{\,\backslash\,})&(P\star Q)\,\backslash\,L&\equiv&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}P\star Q\,\backslash\,L\\
P\,\backslash\,L+Q\,\backslash\,L\\
P\,\backslash\,L\,|\,Q\,\backslash\,L\end{array}\right.&\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{if }\mathcal{L}(P)\cap(L\cup\overline{L})=\{\,\}\\
\mbox{if }\star=+\\
\mbox{if }\star=\,|\,\mbox{ and }\mathcal{L}(P)\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}(Q)}\cap(L\cup\overline{L})=\{\,\}\end{array}\par\par\end{array}

Figure 1:
Structural Congruence, where
\star\in\{\,|\,,+\}
and
\wr\in\{\,\backslash\,,\,/\penalty\,\}
.

Following
Milner’s presentation of the
\pi
-calculus in
[
28
]
, we define a structural congruence over processes.
Specifically,

structural congruence

\equiv
is the smallest congruence over
\mathcal{P}
that satisfies the equations in Fig.
1
.
The idea of the structural congruence is to split interaction rules between agents from rules governing the “left-to-right” representation of expressions.
It allows to use those equations in the derivation rules of the SOS making the latter simpler and easy to use, and it allows to set up formal equivalence of processes increasing for each program the number of legal SOS transitions that, otherwise, would be blocked.
Milner divides
CCS
equations in static, dynamic and expressions laws. We choose an approach inspired by the work of Berry and Boudol
[
7
]
by adding an explicit structural rule in the operational semantics.

Note that we do not include in our structural rules all of Milner’s
\tau
-laws, i.e. the ones related to the presence of
\tau
-actions, like e.g.
\alpha.\tau.P\equiv\alpha.P
, or
P+\tau.P\equiv\tau.P
,
and we have also dropped the
congruence equations related to the uniqueness of solution of recursive equations and the

expansion laws
related to the
standard concurrent form
(cf. Chap. 3, Props. 2, 4(2) and 5 of
[
27
]
).
Since we do not propose notions of behavioural equivalence in this paper, we prefer to take a conservative approach and only consider a minumum set of purely structural rules that do not impinge on the dynamics of expression behaviour.

2.2
Operational Semantics

The operational semantics for
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
is given using a labelled transition relation
P\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}}Q
in the style of Plotkin’s Structured Operational Semantics (SOS)
[
35
,
34
]
, where
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
is the action that labels the SOS transition. When
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}
, the transition corresponds to a communication step, namely a rendezvous action
or
a broadcasted clock action.
When
\alpha=\tau
is silent, then the transition corresponds to a
reduction
or an
evaluation
step.
In addition, we decorate the nondeterministic SOS with annotations that provides sufficient information to express a uniform and confluent reduction strategy based on scheduling precedences.
Including these annotations, our SOS judgment takes the form

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q

where
P,Q,R\in\mathcal{P}
are processes,
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
is an action, and
H\subseteq\mathit{Act}
a set of actions.
The semantics is defined inductively as the smallest relation closed under the rules in Fig.
2
. We call each transition
derivable by Fig.
2
an
admissible
transition. We denote by
\alpha{:}H[R]
a compound
c-action
consisting of the three annotations
\alpha
,
H
, and
R
. The information provided by such a c-action is sufficient to define our
constructive reduction
strategy for
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
that satisfies a refined confluence property. Sometimes, we write
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q
to state that there is a transition for some
R
and
H
.

The intuition behind the annotations of admissible transitions is as follows:
\alpha
is the usual action “passed back” in the SOS. The
blocking set

H
is the set of actions that take precedence over
\alpha
, and
R
, called the
concurrent context
, is a process that represents the behaviour of all threads in
P
that execute concurrently with the transition. i.e., all actions in
R
are potentially in competition with
\alpha
.
Think of
H
as the resources that are required by the action and of
R
as the concurrent context that potentially competes for
H
. In a parallel composition
P\,|\,Q
of
P
with another process
Q
, the context
R

might interact with the environment
Q
to generate actions in the blocking set
H
. If this happens, the reduction
in
P
with blocking set
H
will be considered blocked in our scheduled semantics (see Def.
28
).

In the rules of Fig.
2
, the reader can observe how the annotation for the concurrent context and the blocking sets are inductively built.
In a nutshell:
Rule
(\textit{Act})
is the axiom annotating in the transition the action and the (empty) environment.
Rules
(\textit{Restr})
and
(\textit{Hide}
) deal with “local restriction” of rendezvous actions and “local hiding” of broadcasted clocks.
Rules
(\textit{Par})

and
(\textit{Sum})

are almost standard and deal with parallelism and choice.
Rule
(\textit{Struct})
internalises in the transition the above presented structural equivalence of processes.
Rule
(\textit{Com})
constitutes the core of our
SOS. It captures, in an elegant way, both the classical
CCS
rendezvous communication rule
and
the classical
CSP
broadcasting communication; it has an extra
race
test, enriching the blocking set of the conclusion by
\tau
, in case
P
and
Q
interfere. The presence of
\tau
will block unconditionally.
Finally, rule
(\textit{Con})
deals with process names and refers to a process whose behaviour is specified by a definitional equation
A\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}P
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{2cm}}r}\lx@intercol\alpha{:}H{\mathsf{.}}P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P\qquad\hfill P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q\lx@proof@logical@and P\equiv P^{\prime}P^{\prime}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}Q^{\prime}\equiv QR^{\prime}\equiv R\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.53581pt]
\lx@intercol P\,\backslash\,L\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,\backslash\,L}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}Q\,\backslash\,L\lx@proof@logical@and P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}QL^{\prime}=L\cup\overline{L}\alpha\not\in L^{\prime}H^{\prime}=H-L^{\prime}\qquad P\,/\penalty\,L\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,/\penalty\,L}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha\,/\penalty\,L}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}Q\,/\penalty\,L\lx@proof@logical@and P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}QH^{\prime}=H-L\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.53581pt]
\lx@intercol P\,|\,Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,|\,Q}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\,|\,Q\lx@proof@logical@and P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\alpha\not\in\mathcal{C}\qquad\hfill P+Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[11.38109pt]
\lx@intercol P\,|\,Q\xrightarrow[R_{1}\,|\,R_{2}]{\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}\cup H}}\,P^{\prime}\,|\,Q^{\prime}\lx@proof@logical@and P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P^{\prime}Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\ell}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}H=race(P,Q)\qquad\hfill A\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\lx@proof@logical@and A\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}PP\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.53581pt]
\lx@intercol\textit{where }\hfill\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip 28.45274pt\alpha\,/\penalty\,L\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad\ \ }l}\tau&\text{if }\alpha\in L\\
\alpha&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\quad\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau&\text{if }\ell\in\mathcal{R}\\
\ell&\text{if }\ell\in\mathcal{C}\end{array}\right.\\[11.38109pt]
race(P,Q)\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\tau\}&\mbox{if }H_{1}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(Q)\not\subseteq\{\ell\}\text{ or }H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P)\not\subseteq\{\overline{\ell}\}\\
\{\,\}&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

Figure 2:
The admissible transitions of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
processes. See Def.
2
for the
initial actions

\textsf{iA}(P)
.

2.3
Constructive Scheduling

The operational semantics of Fig.
2
for rendezvous actions coincides with that of
CCS
and the rules for clock actions coincide with the semantics of
CSP
broadcast actions. The scheduling annotations
H
and
R
of a c-action
\alpha{:}H[R]
expose extra information about blocking and concurrent context, respectively, of the underlying action
\alpha
. We can use them to implement different scheduling strategies.
Prioritised process algebras
[
10
,
36
,
33
]
block an admissible transition
\alpha{:}H[R]
when
H
contains the silent action
\tau
or an action that synchronises with some initial action of the concurrent context
R
.
Otherwise, it is (weakly) enabled.

Definition 2

(Initial actions and weakly-enabled transition)
.

•

The set
\textsf{iA}(R)\subseteq\mathit{Act}
of initial actions of a process
R
is given as
\textsf{iA}(R)=\{\alpha\mid\exists Q.\,R\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q\}
.

•

An transition with c-action
\alpha{:}H[R]

is called weakly enabled if
H\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R)\cup\{\tau\})=\{\,\}
.

Let us call a process
P

free
if
all its c-actions
\alpha{:}H[R]
have an empty blocking set
H=\{\,\}
. The weakly enabled transitions of (unclocked) free processes coincide with the semantics of
CCS

[
27
]
. We call
P

discrete
if all its c-actions
\alpha{:}H[R]
are self-blocking, i.e.
H=\{\alpha\}
.
A process
P
is
irreflexive
if no action prefix
\alpha{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
occurring in
P
blocks itself, i.e.,
\alpha\not\in H
.
One can show that for (unclocked) irreflexive processes, a transition is weakly enabled iff is derivable in the operational semantics of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}

[
33
]
2
2

2

In
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
the blocking sets are written before the action,
H{:}\alpha{\mathsf{.}}P
, where we write
\alpha{:}H{\mathsf{.}}P
in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
.
.
Thus,
CCS
and
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
correspond to theories of free and irreflexive processes of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, respectively, under weak enabling.
Likewise the theories
[
10
,
36
]
can be subsumed; specifically, the
prioritised sum
operator
P\mathrel{+\hskip-4.2679pt\rangle}Q
of
[
10
]

can be coded as
P+Q{:}\textsf{iA}(P)
in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
.
The theory
[
36
]
proposes two
levels
of labels,
a{:}0
and
a{:}1
, where
0
denotes higher priority and
1
ordinary priority.
Translated into
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, the prefix
a{:}0{\mathsf{.}}P
corresponds to
a{:}\{\,\}{\mathsf{.}}P

while an action prefix
a{:}1{\mathsf{.}}P
maps in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
to
a{:}\mathsf{Prio}{\mathsf{.}}P
if we define
\sf Prio
as the set of all high-prioritised labels.

Examples
4
and
5
below in Sec.
3
demonstrate how prioritised scheduling by weak enabling (Def.
2
) can indeed help to ensure determinacy in special cases. For the general case, however, weak enabling is not sufficient.
In order to prevent non-determinacy (a global property) from priorities (a purely local property),
we must be able to block a c-action
\alpha{:}H[R]
not just if the initial actions
\textsf{iA}(R)
of the concurrent context
R
can synchronise with
H
, as in weak enabling, but look at the set
\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(R)
of all actions that
R
can potentially engage in, before it reaches a clock.

Example
7
in Sec.
3
illustrates this.

Definition 3

(Potential actions and constructive enabling)
.

•

The set
\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{L}
of potential actions is the smallest extension
\textsf{iA}(P)\cap\mathcal{L}\subseteq\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)
such that if
\ell\in\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(Q)
and
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q
for
\alpha\in\mathcal{R}\cup\{\tau\}
, then
\ell\in\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)
.

•

A transition with c-action
\alpha{:}H[R]
is called constructively enabled, or c-enabled for short, if
H\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R)\cup\{\tau\})=\{\,\}
.

The following Sec.
3
presents some motivating examples, while Sec.
4
focuses
on the mathematical theory underlying
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
.

3
Examples

The first example shows the basic mechanism of how priorities can be used to schedule processes under weak enabling.

Example 4

(Read Before Write)
.

Consider a “store”
S\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}w{\mathsf{.}}r+r{:}w
that offers a write action
w
followed by a read
r
, or a read action
r
but prefers the write over the read. The fact that
w
takes priority over
r
is recorded in the blocking set
\{w\}
which contains the action
w
. If we put
S
in parallel with a reader
R\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{r}
, the read actions
r
and
\overline{r}
synchronise in a reduction

S\,|\,R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}.
The blocking set
\{w\}
will block
R
’s transition when a parallel writer
W\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{w}
is added, as well. Specifically,
the reduction
S\,|\,R\,|\,W\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{w}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w\}}}\,$}W

is not weakly enabled, because
\{w\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(\overline{w})=\{w\}\cap\{w\}\neq\{\,\}
.
The only enabled reduction is that between
S
and
W
giving

S\,|\,R\,|\,W\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{r}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w\}}}\,$}r\,|\,R

and only then continue with the read
r\,|\,R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}
. Thus, weak enabling makes the reduction determinate.
Observe that
S
is not confluent (Def.
1
):
We have
S\xrightarrow{w}r
and
S\xrightarrow{r}{{\mathsf{0}}}
but there is no
P_{1}\equiv P_{2}
such that
r\xrightarrow{r}P_{1}
and
{{\mathsf{0}}}\xrightarrow{w}P_{2}
. However,
S
is coherent (Def.
12
), because the transition
S\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{r}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}
interferes (Def.
11
) with the former
S\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{w}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\}}}\,$}r
as
w\in\{w\}
, whence coherence does not require confluence.

Processes can block each other from making progress. This happens under weak enabling if processes offer two-way rendezvous synchronisation with contradicting precedences.

Example 5

(Binary Blocking)
.

Take processes
P\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a{:}b{\mathsf{.}}A+b
and
Q\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{b}{:}\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}B+\overline{a}
.
Their composition
P\,|\,Q
has two admissible reductions, through
a{:}b\,|\,\overline{a}
to
A
or through
b\,|\,\overline{b}{:}\overline{a}
to
B
.
So, disregarding the precedences, it is non-deterministic if
A\not\equiv B
. Under weak enabling, however,
P\,|\,Q
creates a circular deadlock:
P
offers
a
unless synchronisation with
\overline{b}
is possible, while
Q
offers
\overline{b}
provided there is no synchronisation with
a
. None of the reductions is enabled. By the “race” side-condition of
(\textit{Com})
the synchronisation
a{:}b\,|\,\overline{a}
generates the c-action
\tau{:}H[{{\mathsf{0}}}]
with
\tau\in H
because
\{b\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(Q)\not\subseteq\{a\}
. Similarly, since
\{\overline{a}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P)\not\subseteq\{\overline{b}\}
, the synchronisation
b\,|\,\overline{b}{:}\overline{a}
is blocked.
We note that neither
P
nor
Q
is confluent, but they are coherent.

Example 6

(Reflexive Blocking)
.

Consider the free process
s\,|\,\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}A\,|\,\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}B
where action
s
can be consumed by
\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}A
or by
\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}B
, generating non-deterministic
\tau
-transitions to
A\,|\,\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}B
or to
\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}A\,|\,B
. However, by adding self-blocking, as in
s{:}s\,|\,\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}A\,|\,\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}B
, weak enabling protects the prefix
s
from being consumed when both
\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}A
and by
\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}B
compete for it. If only one is present, as in
s{:}s\,|\,\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}A
and
s{:}s\,|\,\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}B
, no blocking occurs.

Blocking under weak enabling ensures determinism in the special case where the circular dependency involves only initial actions. However, parallel threads can create dependency chains through sequences of actions, e.g., when accessing a shared process that is acting as a resource. This is the general case handled by c-enabling.

Example 7

(Transitive Blocking)
.

Consider the process

S\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}w_{0}+r_{0}{:}w_{0}\,|\,w_{1}+r_{1}{:}w_{1}
which offers receiver actions
w_{i}
and
r_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
such that
w_{i}
has precedence over
r_{i}
.
Now take processes
P_{0}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{r_{0}}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w_{1}}
and
P_{1}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{r_{1}}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w_{0}}
which first aim to synchronise with
r_{i}
and then sequentially afterwards with
w_{1-i}
. The parallel composition
P_{0}\,|\,S\,|\,P_{1}
has two initial
\tau
-reductions

P_{0}\,|\,w_{0}+r_{0}{:}w_{0}\,|\,\overline{w_{0}}\xleftarrow[\raisebox{-1.68887pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{P_{0}\,|\,(w_{0}+r_{0}{:}w_{0})}$}]{\tau}\!\!{{}_{\{w_{1}\}}}\,P_{0}\,|\,S\,|\,P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{w_{1}+r_{1}{:}w_{1}\,|\,P_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w_{0}\}}}\,$}\overline{w_{1}}\,|\,w_{1}+r_{1}{:}w_{1}\,|\,P_{1}
.
These reductions are both weakly enabled. If we continue under weak enabling then the residual process
\overline{w_{1}}\,|\,w_{1}+r_{1}{:}w_{1}\,|\,P_{1}
for the first reduction must first execute the synchronisation on
w_{1}
and thus proceed as
\overline{w_{1}}\,|\,w_{1}+r_{1}{:}w_{1}\,|\,P_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}}P_{1}
while the residual process
P_{0}\,|\,w_{0}+r_{0}{:}w_{0}\,|\,\overline{w_{0}}
can only reduce to
P_{0}
. Hence, the resulting final outcome of executing
P_{0}\,|\,S\,|\,P_{1}
is non-deterministic.
Observe that none of the two reductions displayed above is actually c-enabled. For the right reduction we find that its concurrent environment
R_{0}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}w_{1}+r_{1}{:}w_{1}\,|\,P_{1}
has a reduction sequence
R_{0}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}}\overline{w_{0}}
and thus

\{w_{0}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{0})\subseteq\{w_{0}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(\overline{w_{0}})=\{w_{0}\}\cap\{w_{0}\}\neq\{\,\}.
Symmetrically, for the concurrent environment
R_{1}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}P_{0}\,|\,w_{0}+r_{0}{:}w_{0}
of the left reduction we have
R_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}}\overline{w_{1}}
and hence
\{w_{1}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{1})\neq\{\,\}
. The parallel composition
P_{0}\,|\,S\,|\,P_{1}
is deterministic under c-enabling. We note again,
S
is not confluent but coherent (Def.
12
).

The next example generalises Ex.
4
to a full memory cell.

Example 8

(Read/Write Memory)
.

Let
w_{0},w_{1},r_{0},r_{1}
the labels of writing/reading a boolean value
0
or
1
, respectively. A memory would be modelled by two mutually recursive processes

\displaystyle\mathsf{wAND}_{0}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w_{1}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{wAND}_{1}+w_{0}{:}w_{1}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{wAND}_{0}+r_{0}{:}\{w_{0},w_{1}\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{wAND}_{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{wAND}_{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w_{1}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{wAND}_{1}+w_{0}{:}w_{1}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{wAND}_{0}+r_{1}{:}\{w_{0},w_{1}\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{wAND}_{1}

The process
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}
represents the memory cell in a state where it has stored the value
v\in\{0,1\}
. In either state, it offers to synchronise with a write action
w_{u}
to change its state to
\mathsf{wAND}_{u}
, and also with a read action
r_{v}
to transmit its stored value
v
to a reader. The write actions
w_{u}
do not only change the state of the memory, they also block the read actions since they appear in their blocking sets
r_{v}{:}\{w_{0},w_{1}\}
.

Between the write actions, the blocking sets implement a precedence of
w_{1}
over
w_{0}
. In the picture on the right the process state transitions are visualised with the blocking sets as
\color
red red dashed arrows.

Example 9

(Esterel Signals)
.

Concurrent Esterel threads communicate via signals
[
6
,
38
]
. A (pure, temporary) signal can have two statuses, present and absent. At the beginning of a synchronous macro-step, each signal is initialised to be absent, by default. The signal becomes present as soon as some thread emits it. It remains present thereafter for throughout the current macro-step, i.e., until the next clock cycle is started. The clock is a global synchronisation, taken by all threads simultaneously. The value of the signal is broadcast to all concurrent threads which can test it and branch their control-flow according to the signal’s status. To maintain coherency of data-flow and deterministic behaviour, the signal status can only be read when no writing is possible any more. Esterel compilers conduct a causality analysis on the program and obtain a suitable static schedule when they generate imperative code. In Esterel hardware compilers, the scheduling is achieved dynamically by
propagating signal statuses. Programs that cannot be scheduled to satisfy the emit-before-test protocol, or hardware that that does not stabilise are called non-constructive and rejected by the compiler. A pure, temporary signal is modelled by the following recursive behaviour:

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathit{abs}{:}\mathit{emit}{\mathsf{.}}S_{0}+\mathit{emit}{\mathsf{.}}S_{1}+\sigma{:}\{\mathit{abs},\mathit{emit}\}{\mathsf{.}}S_{0}

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathit{pres}{\mathsf{.}}S_{1}+\mathit{emit}{\mathsf{.}}S_{1}+\sigma{:}\{\mathit{pres},\mathit{emit}\}{\mathsf{.}}S_{0}

which is depicted on the right
and structurally coherent according to Def.
12
.

Example 10

(
\mathsf{ABRO}
)
.

The hello word in synchronous languages, see
[
6
]
,
\mathsf{ABRO}
has the following behaviour:
Emit the output
o
as soon as both inputs
a
and
b
have been received. Reset this behaviour each time the input
r
occurs, without emitting
o
in the reset cycle.
[
38
]

The behaviour of
\mathsf{ABRO}
is expressed as a Mealy machine and a SyncChart, displayed in Fig.
3
on the right. The synchronous behaviour of Esterel lies in the assumption that each transition of the Mealy machine summarises a single macro-step interaction of the machine with its environment.
The transitions of parallel machines synchronise so that state changes proceed in lock-step. The simultaneous execution of these transitions that make up a macro-step consists of the sending and receiving of signals. The propagation of individual signals between

machines is modeled in the micro-step operational semantics of Esterel.
In
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
we represent the micro-steps as sequences of
\tau
-transitions, i.e., rendez-vous synchronisations, whereas the macro-step is captured as a global clock synchronisation. Unfortunately, the Mealy machine formalism is not scalable because the number of states can grow exponentially as the number of signals grow, while in Esterel can be expressed in a linear number of code lines as seen on the right.

module ABRO:
input A, B, R;
output O;
loop
await A || await B;
emit O
each R
end module

The

loop P each R
construct of Esterel executes the behaviour of program
P
for an unbounded number of clock cycles, but restarts its body
P
when the signal
R
becomes present. The reset
R
takes priority over the behaviour of
P
which is preempted at the moment that the reset occurs. Note that in Esterel each signal appears exactly once, as in the specification and unlike in the Mealy machine.
Other parts of Esterel also naturally arise from precedence-based scheduling. Esterel’s
loop P each R
mechanism, for instance, can be coded using the static parallel composition operator that combines the process
P
to be aborted and reset with a “watchdog” process
R
. The latter waits for the reset signal from the environment in each clock cycle. The choice between the reset and continuing inside
P
is resolved by giving
R
higher priority. When the reset occurs, the watchdog sends “kill” signals to all threads running in
P
and waits for these to terminate. Only then the watchdog
R
restarts
P
from scratch. This is a form of precedence that can be expressed in the blocking sets of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
. As such, Fig.
3
on the left translates the Esterel code of
\mathsf{ABRO}
compositionally in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{ABRO}&\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}&\sigma{\mathsf{.}}(\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B}\,|\,\mathsf{R}\,|\,\mathsf{O}\,|\,\mathsf{T})\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{A}&\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}&k_{a}{:}k_{a}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+a{:}\{k_{a},a\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{s}{:}\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+\sigma{:}\{k_{a},a\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{A}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{B}&\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}&k_{b}{:}k_{b}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+b{:}\{k_{b},b\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{s}{:}\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+\sigma{:}\{k_{b},b\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{B}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{R}&\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}&r{:}r{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{a}{:}\overline{k}_{a}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{b}{:}\overline{k}_{b}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{s}{:}\overline{k}_{s}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{t}{:}\overline{k}_{t}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{ABRO}+\tau{:}{r}{\mathsf{.}}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{R}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{O}&\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}&k_{t}{:}k_{t}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+t{:}\{k_{t},t\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{o}{:}\overline{o}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+\sigma{:}\{k_{t},t\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{O}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{T}&\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}&k_{s}{:}k_{s}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+s{:}\{k_{s}\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{T}+\overline{t}{:}\{k_{s},\overline{t},s\}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+\sigma{:}\{k_{s},\overline{t},s\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{T}\par\end{array}

Figure 3
:
\mathsf{ABRO}
as a
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
process, Mealy machine and SyncChart.

\mathsf{ABRO}
is obtained as a parallel composition
(\mathsf{R}\,|\,\mathsf{ABO})\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}
combining a watchdog
\mathsf{R}
with the behaviour
\mathsf{ABO}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B}\,|\,\mathsf{O}\,|\,{\mathsf{T}}
. The
\mathsf{R}
is responsible to implement the reset loop at the superstate called
\mathsf{ABO}
, as in the state charts.
The
\mathsf{ABO}
is the behaviour of the interior of this superstate, which corresponds to the statement
(await A || await B); emit O
in Esterel:
it is the parallel composition of
\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B}
making up the behaviour of the region called
\mathsf{AB}
with code
await A || await B
. The processes
\mathsf{T}\,|\,\mathsf{O}
implement the synchronisation that waits for termination of
\mathsf{AB}
to start the output process
\mathsf{O}
.
We assume that the channels
a
,
b
,
r
,
o
modelling interface signals
A
,
B
,
R
,
O

all have a unique sender and receiver.
This is seen in our coding from the fact that the prefixes for these channels are self-blocking.

4
Coherence and Determinacy for
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}

This section presents the core elements
of our scheduling theory under constructive enabling: coherence, policies, and policy-conformant processes.

Coherence
(Def.
12
) corresponds to Milner’s notion of confluence, but refined “up-to” priorities. It requires structural confluence for c-enabled and diverging c-actions only if they do not interfere with each other considering their blocking sets and concurrent environments.

Definition 11

(Transition Interference)
.

Two c-enabled transitions with c-actions
\alpha_{1}{:}H_{1}[E_{1}]
and
\alpha_{2}{:}H_{2}[E_{2}]

are interference-free if
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{i}\not\in H_{3-i}
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Observe that for free processes (i.e., with empty blocking sets) all c-actions are interference-free and they are c-enabled iff they are admissible.

Confluence of interference-free c-actions is formulated using an auxiliary form of
residual transition

P\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}Q
. This is a transition from
P
to
Q
with an action that is a “factor” of
\alpha
. Formally, we define
P\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}Q
if
(i)

\alpha\in\mathcal{L}
with
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q
or
P\equiv Q
, or
(ii)

\alpha=\tau
with
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q
or
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q
or
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\ell}}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q
for some
\ell\in\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 12

(Process Coherence)
.

A process
P
is (structurally) coherent if for all its derivatives
Q
the following holds:
Given two c-enabled and interference-free transitions

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}Q_{1}\text{ and }Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}Q_{2}
such that
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
or
Q_{1}\not\equiv Q_{2}
or both
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
and
\alpha_{i}\not\in H_{3-i}
.
Then, there exist transitions

Q_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{2}^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}\text{ and }Q_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{1}^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}
with
H_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{i}
and

E_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}E_{1}^{\prime}
and
E_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}E_{2}^{\prime}
.

For free processes, coherence implies confluence (Def.
1
), but is strictly stronger.

Example 13
.

For instance,
Q\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a{:}\{\,\}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
is confluent but not coherent. Since the prefix not self-blocking, coherence ensures that the label must consumable by arbitrarily many concurrent processes. Technically, since
Q\xrightarrow{a}Q_{1}
and
Q\xrightarrow{a}Q_{2}
for

Q_{1}\equiv{{\mathsf{0}}}\equiv Q_{2}
, there should be a transition
Q_{i}\xrightarrow{a}Q^{\prime}
which is not the case. However, for discrete processes, coherence is identical with confluence: With self-blocking,
Q\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a{:}\{a\}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
is both confluent and coherent. It is important to note that all examples in Sec.
3
are coherent but not confluent.

Theorem 14

(Coherence implies Determinacy)
.

Every coherent process is determinate under
c-enabled reductions.

A coherent process cannot simultaneously offer both a clock and another distinct action without putting them in precedence order. Moreover, whenever a clock is enabled, then every further reduction of the process is blocked by the clock.

Thm.
14
concerns the
\tau
-transitions of a coherent process. For clock transitions we have an even stronger result.

Proposition 15

(Clock Determinism)
.

Every coherent process

P
is structurally clock deterministic, i.e., if

P\xrightarrow{\sigma}P_{1}\text{ and }P\xrightarrow{\sigma}P_{2}
then
P_{1}\equiv P_{2}
.

The question is now how we can obtain coherent processes by composition. In view of Milner’s classical result for confluence, we expect to have to impose some form of “sort-separation” for parallel processes.
It turns out that we can express such a condition and even avoid the restriction operator (thereby permit sharing) if we enrich the sorts
\mathcal{L}(P)
,
\mathcal{L}(Q)
by priority information. We call the resulting enriched sorts
\pi(P)
,
\pi(Q)

precedence policies
.

Definition 16

(Precedence Policy)
.

A precedence policy is a pair
\pi=(L,\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$})
where
L\subseteq\mathcal{L}
is a subset of visible actions, called the alphabet of
\pi
and
\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\subseteq L\times L
a binary relation on
L
, called the precedence relation.

We will write
\ell\in\pi
to state that
\ell
is in the alphabet of
\pi
and we write
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi
to express that the pair
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is
in the precedence relation of the policy.
Further, if
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\pi
and both
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\not\in\pi
as well as
\ell_{2}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{1}\not\in\pi
, we say that
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are
concurrently independent
under
\pi
, written (by abuse of the set-notation)
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}\in\pi
.

For any policy
\pi=(L,\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$})
and set of labels
K\subseteq\mathcal{L}
we denote by
\pi\,\backslash\,K
the policy
\pi
restricted to the alphabet
L-(K\cup\overline{K})
in the standard way.
There is also the normal inclusion ordering
\pi_{1}\subseteq\pi_{2}
between policies defined by
L_{1}\subseteq L_{2}
and
\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{1}$}\subseteq\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{2}$}
.

The next Def.
17
on conformance captures how policies statically specify the externally observable scheduling behaviour of a process.

Definition 17

(Conformance)
.

P
conforms to a policy
\pi
if for each derivative
Q
of
P
: If

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}

with
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
, then
\ell\in\pi
and for all
\ell^{\prime}\in H\cap\mathcal{L}
, then

\ell^{\prime}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\pi
.

For a process
P
and policy
\pi
, let us write
3
3

3

In Milner’s notation we would write
P:\pi
, but we already use the colon ‘:’ for blocking sets as in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
.

P\Vdash\pi
to express that
P
is coherent and conforms to policy
\pi
. We will say that
P
is
coherent for

\pi
.
If
P\Vdash\pi
then the alphabet of
\pi
corresponds to the sort
\mathcal{L}(P)
in
CCS
. It gives an upper bound on the labels that can be used by the process, and the precedence relation in
\pi
provides a static over-approximation of the blocking, i.e., which labels can be in a choice conflict with each other. As a notational shortcut we will write
P\Vdash\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}
to say that there is a policy
\pi
with
P\Vdash\pi
and
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi
. Analogously, we write
P\Vdash\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\ell_{2}
if there is a policy
\pi
with
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\ell_{2}\in\pi
and
P\Vdash\pi
.

Example 18
.

The most permissive policy for a given sort
L\subseteq\mathcal{L}
, written
\pi_{max}(L)
, contains all labels from
L
as its alphabet but no precedences, i.e.,
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\ell_{2}\in\pi_{max}(L)
for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L
. If
P\Vdash\pi_{max}
then
P
has sort
L
and will permit arbitrarily many concurrent processes to consume its labels
L
under c-enabling.
The most restrictive policy, denoted
\pi_{min}(L)
, also has alphabet
L
, but full precedences, i.e.,
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi_{min}
for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L
. If
P\Vdash\pi_{min}(L)
, then
P
has sort
L
but does not permit any concurrency; it can only communicate with a single sequential thread.

Example 19
.

Reflexive precedences
\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\pi
play a special role in our theory. They permit a conformant
P\Vdash\pi
and its derivatives to execute
\ell
only with a single partner. For instance,
\mathsf{ABRO}
from Ex.
10
satisfies
\mathsf{ABRO}\nVdash r\mathrel{\diamond}r
and so
\mathsf{ABRO}\,|\,\sigma{\mathsf{.}}\overline{r}
reduces but
\mathsf{ABRO}\,|\,\sigma{\mathsf{.}}\overline{r}\,|\,\sigma{\mathsf{.}}\overline{r}
will block after the initial clock transition.
In contrast, if the policy specifies
\ell\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell\in\pi
, then in a process
P\Vdash\pi
any
\ell
-transition is consumable arbitrarily often. For instance, the Esterel signal from Ex.
9
has
S_{1}\Vdash\mathit{pres}\mathrel{\diamond}\mathit{pres}
and so
S_{1}\,|\,\overline{pres}\,|\,\overline{\mathit{pres}}
will not block.

Here we are interested in
processes that conform to policies with special properties.

Definition 20

(Pivot and precedence-closed policy)
.

A policy
\pi
is called

•

pivotable if
\overline{L}\subseteq L
and for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L
with
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
we have
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}\in\pi
or
\overline{\ell}_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\pi
.

•

precedence-closed for
L\subseteq\mathcal{L}
if
\ell_{1}\in L
and
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi
implies
\ell_{2}\in L
.

A process is pivotable/precedence-closed if
P
conforms to pivot/precedence-closed policy.

Example 21
.

An Esterel signal (Ex.
9
) has the alphabet
\{\mathit{emit},\mathit{pres},\mathit{abs}\}=\mathcal{L}(S_{i})
(
i\in\{1,2\}
and for simplicity we ignore the clock
\sigma
) and is conformant to the policy
\mathsf{psig}
with
\mathit{emit}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\mathit{abs}\in\mathsf{psig}
.
A typical program
R
accessing the signal has alphabet
\{\overline{\mathit{pres}},\overline{\mathit{abs}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(S_{i})
and is of form
R=\overline{\mathit{pres}}{:}\overline{\mathit{pres}}{\mathsf{.}}R_{1}+\overline{\mathit{abs}}{:}\{\overline{\mathit{abs}},\overline{\mathit{pres}}\}{\mathsf{.}}R_{0}
. If the signal is present then
R_{1}
is executed, if it is absent then
R_{0}
is executed.
Such
R
conforms to a policy
\mathsf{prg}
with
\overline{\mathit{pres}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\mathit{abs}}\in\mathsf{prg}
and
\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\mathsf{prg}
for
\ell\in\{\overline{\mathit{pres}},\overline{abs}\}
. Both
\mathsf{psig}
and
\mathsf{prg}
as well as their union
\mathsf{psig}\cup\mathsf{prg}
are pivot policies, so that
R\,|\,S_{i}
is pivotable.
In a pivotable process, conflicting choices are resolved by the precedences in a single thread, acting as the “pivot”. In
R\,|\,S_{i}
the pivot is the signal
S_{i}
on reading, resolving the choice
\overline{\mathit{pres}}+\overline{\mathit{abs}}
in
R
, and it is the program
R
on writing, resolving the choice
\mathit{abs}+\mathit{emit}
in
S_{i}
.

Non-pivotable processes, in general, may block because of two threads entering into a circular precedence deadlock with each other.

Example 22
.

For example, recall the processes
P\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a{:}b{\mathsf{.}}A+b
and
Q\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{b}{:}\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}B+\overline{a}
from Example
5
.
Their parallel composition
P\,|\,Q
creates a deadlock under (even weak) enabling because of the contradicting precedences in the two possible interactions
a{:}b\,|\,\overline{a}
and
b\,|\,\overline{b}{:}\overline{a}
.
The deadlock is reflected in the policy
\pi
of
P
satisfying
b\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}a\in\pi
and
\overline{a}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{b}\in\pi
which is not a pivot policy that would instead require
a\mathrel{\diamond}b\in\pi
or
\overline{a}\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{b}\in\pi
.

Reductions of pivotable processes never block because of two threads entering into a circular precedence deadlock with each other.
One can show that for pivotable processes of at most two threads, the three operational semantics of
CCS
(admissible), of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
(weak enabling) and our
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
(c-enabling) all coincide.
This is a consequence of
the fact (cf. Lem.
C.56
and
C.58
) that
the race test in the rule
(\textit{Com})
is never introducing the silent action
\tau
into the blocking sets.

In general, pivotable processes may consist of three or more interacting threads, and blocking may occur and the semantics of
CCS
,
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
are different.
However, one can show that the blocking of a synchronisation is always due to the external environment.
In other words, the computation of
race(P,Q)
in rule
(\textit{Com})
becomes redundant. Two single-threaded pivotable processes never block each other.

Pivotable process cannot offer a clock action and exhibit reduction at the same time. This means that in this class of processes, clocks and reductions are sequentially scheduled.

Proposition 23

(Clock Maximal Progress)
.

Suppose
P
is coherent and pivotable. If
\sigma\in\textsf{iA}(P)
then
P
is in normal form, i.e., there is no reduction
P\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}}P^{\prime}
.

Prop.
23
can be seen as saying that in pivotable processes all clocks take lowest precedence. As a result, clock transitions satisfy
maximal progress
, i.e., a clock is only enabled on normal forms, i.e., if there is no reduction possible. Thus clocks behave like time steps in the standard timed process algebras
[
18
,
11
]
. More importantly (Thm.
12
), all reductions are confluent. So, a pivotable process, no matter in which order the reductions are executed, when it terminates, it computes a unique normal form. At this point it either stops if there is no clock possible, or it offers a clock step (pausing) to a continuation process from which a new normal form is produced. In this way, coherent pivotable processes correspond to synchronous streams.

It remains to be seen how we can obtain coherent processes systematically by construction. The following Thm.
24
identifies a set of closure operators for coherent processes based on policy-conformance.
This is our main theorem that summarises Propositions
D.74
–
D.86
(Appendix), which

provide in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
a generalisation of Milner’s Confluence Class, Prop. 15-17 in
[
27
]
.

Theorem 24

(Milner’s Confluence Class Generalised)
.

{bracketenumerate}

(Idling)
{{\mathsf{0}}}\Vdash\pi
for all policies
\pi
.

(Rendezvous Prefix) If
Q\Vdash\pi
, then for every
\ell\in\mathcal{R}
we have
\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q\Vdash\pi
if
\ell\in H\subseteq\{\ell^{\prime}\mid\ell^{\prime}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\pi\}
.

(Clock Prefix) If
Q\Vdash\pi
and clock
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\sigma{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q\Vdash\pi
if
H\subseteq\{\beta\mid\beta\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\sigma\in\pi\}
.

(Choice) If
P_{1}\Vdash\pi_{1}
and
P_{2}\Vdash\pi_{2}
and for all pairs of initial transitions

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{F_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P_{1}
and

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{F_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}P_{2}

such that
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
as well as
\alpha_{1}\in H_{2}
or
\alpha_{2}\in H_{1}
.
Then
P_{1}+P_{2}\Vdash\pi
if
\pi_{1}\subseteq\pi
and
\pi_{2}\subseteq\pi
.

(Parallel) If
Q\Vdash\pi
and
R\Vdash\pi
where
\pi\,\backslash\,\mathcal{R}
is a pivot policy, then
(Q\,|\,R)\Vdash\pi
.

(Hiding) If
Q\Vdash\pi
and
\sigma\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\pi
for all
\sigma\in L
and
\ell\in\pi
, then
Q\,/\penalty\,L\Vdash\pi
.

(Restriction) If
Q\Vdash\pi
and
\pi
precedence-closed for
L\cup\overline{L}
, then
({Q}\,\backslash\,{L})\Vdash\pi\,\backslash\,L
.

The theorem is the main result of the paper, namely that under a suitable “policy decoration”, a process can be run deterministically in the scheduled SOS semantics of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
.

Example 25
.

To give a concrete example of the property stated by the above theorem, let’s revisit Ex.
10
: the processes
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{R}
,
\mathsf{O}
,
\mathsf{T}
are all coherent and conform to the pivot policy
\pi_{\mathsf{ABRO}}
with precedences (ignoring the clock, so
\pi\setminus\mathcal{R}=\pi
)

k_{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{t}
,
s\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{t}
,

k_{x}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\{k_{x},x\}
for
x\in\{a,b,s,t\}
and

\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell
for
\ell\in\{r,a,b,\overline{s},t,\overline{t},\overline{o}\}
.
Thus, by Thm.
24
(5), their composition satisfies
\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B}\,|\,\mathsf{R}\,|\,\mathsf{O}\,|\,\mathsf{T}\Vdash\pi_{\mathsf{ABRO}}
. Since the policy is also precedence-closed for
\{s,t,\overline{s},\overline{t}\}
, we have
\mathsf{ABRO}\Vdash\pi_{\mathsf{ABRO}}^{\prime}
by Thm.
24
(3,7), where
\pi_{\mathsf{ABRO}}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathsf{ABRO}}\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}
.

Note that, specifically, Thm.
24
(5) is our replacement of Milner’s notion of confluent composition
(P\,|\,Q)\,\backslash\,L
. Finally, to highlight our claim that we extend the classical result, we offer the following definition and conjecture to catch Milner’s setting of
CCS
in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
.

Definition 26

(Milner’s Confluence Class)
.

Milner’s confluence class
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
is the set of processes
P
such that for all derivatives
Q
of
P
, if
Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}
, the following holds:

•

If
\alpha\neq\tau
then
H=\{\alpha\}
and
\alpha\not\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)
.

•

If
\alpha=\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}
then
H=\{\}
and
