\{\ell,\overline{\ell}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)=\{\}
.

Conjecture 1

(Milner’s Confluence Class is Coherent)
.

For all
P,Q\in\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
:

•

P
is coherent iff
P
is confluent.

•

A transition of
P
is c-enabled iff it is admissible.

•

If
\mathcal{L}(P)\cap\mathcal{L}(Q)=\{\,\}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}(P)}\cap\mathcal{L}(Q)=L\cup\overline{L}
, then
(P\,|\,Q)\,\backslash\,L\in\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
.

5
Further work

We list in spare order some possible future works:

•

We plan to study the interaction of our notion of coherence with forms of congruence (static, dynamic and interaction laws in Milner’s jargon) and
notions of bisimulation.

•

We plan to generalise the interaction model of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
to synchronisation algebras permitting action fusion in the spirit of Milner’s ASCCS and Austry and Boudol’s Meije (see
[
26
,
3
]
).

•

Thm.
24
suggests using policies as types. Among possible type disciplines we just mention: behavioural type systems, including session-types to capture rendezvous interactions; intersection-types, including non-idempotent ones to characterise termination and resources awareness, and linear-types to model consuming resources.

•

C-actions are somewhat related with continuation passing style, and, in some sense they are more powerful because they permit a “forecast” analysis of all possible future actions: studying this relation, possibly in relation with abstract interpretation techniques, would be worth of study.

•

Clocks are unordered in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
: introducing a partial order could be an interesting, although economic and fine grained, way to reason about time and time priorities.

Appendix A
From Asynchronous Priorities to Synchronous ones, via Clocks

This rather informal section gives to the curious reader a nice view of our personal pilgrimage of previous work looking at reaching more control and determinism in asynchronous process algebra. This trip lead us to consider adding clocks to the previous literature and reach confluence in a synchronous setting.

A.1
CCS
with (Weak) Priorities

Let us look in more detail on how a precedence mechanism such as “write-before-read” might be derived from the classical priorities studied for
CCS
.
As a simple example, suppose
S
is a (synchronous write, asynchronous read) memory cell that can be read at any time offering the read action
r
, but only be written once with the write action
w
.
Ignoring data values, the cell would be modelled in plain
CCS
by the equations

S\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}w{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}+r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}
where

S^{\prime}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}
and

S^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}+e{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}
.

Figure 4:
Symmetric Choice. The final outcome, in states ③ and ④, is non-determinate because it depends on the order of read and write. The continuations states are
S^{\prime}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}+e{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}
. For confluence, the error state ③ should permit a (delayed) read to move forward and (eventually) meet with state ④, indicated by the dashed red arrow. However, this is not in general guaranteed.

Initially, memory cell
S
is empty and offers a choice between a read action
r
and a write action
w
. If the write action is performed, the cell receives a value and changes to state
S^{\prime}
. In this filled state
S^{\prime}
, only read actions are admissible to access the stored value, the state does not change.
When the read action is executed on the empty cell
S
, however, the continuation state is
S^{\prime\prime}
which represents an error state. In the error state
S^{\prime\prime}
only a read or an error action
e
is possible, but no write and the cell remains in error.
Observe that only the read action is always admissible in both states
S
and
S^{\prime}
, while the write is admissible only in state
S
.
If
S
is put in parallel with a reader offering
\overline{r}
and a writer offering
\overline{w}
, then the final state of
S
depends on
the order between the
\overline{r}
and
\overline{w}
actions. If we synchronise with the external sequence
\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w}
on
S
where the read action is first, the writer gets blocked on the error state
S^{\prime\prime}
,
while if we exercise
\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{r}
where the write action is first, both can proceed and we obtain filled state
S^{\prime}
. In term rewriting system jargon
[
21
]
this is called “critical pairs”.

Formally, let
R\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
be a reader process with a single read and
W\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
the writer with a single write, both terminating in the empty behaviour
{{\mathsf{0}}}
.
Then, according to the operational semantics of
CCS
, we have the two execution sequences

R\,|\,S\,|\,W\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,W

and

R\,|\,S\,|\,W\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}R\,|\,S^{\prime}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,S^{\prime}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}
,
where both configurations
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,W
and

{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}
are distinct normal forms, in the sense that no reductions are possible anymore.
This is pictorially illustrated in Fig.
4
.
Observe that the behaviour is not only
non-deterministic
in the sense that there is more than one scheduling sequence. It is also non-determinate as the final outcome depends on the scheduling decisions.

Among many
CCS
extension with priorities, we looked the one of Camilleri and Winskell
[
10
]
, named
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
in the sequel.
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
provides a
prioritised choice
operator

Q\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\alpha_{1}.Q_{1}\mathrel{+\hskip-4.2679pt\rangle}\alpha_{2}.Q_{2}

called “prisum” that gives the action
\alpha_{1}
priority over
\alpha_{2}
and blocks the second prefix
\alpha_{2}
in case
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
.
Such priorities help us resolve the issue.
The problem with non-determinism originates from the preemptive behaviour of the write-read choice
w{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}+r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}
. It permits the read and the write actions to compete with each other for the next state of the process
S
. In data-flow and functional programming, we resolve the conflict by making the write action take precedence over the read one. First, the data-flow variable must be written by the
producer
process, then it can be read by the
consumers
, if any. This “write-before-read” scheduling policy is a
prioritised choice
, represented in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
as

S\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}w{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}\mathrel{+\hskip-4.2679pt\rangle}r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}

in which the prisum
\mathrel{+\hskip-4.2679pt\rangle}
operator enforces a precedence from left to right. The store
S
is willing to engage in a read action
r
(to the right of
\mathrel{+\hskip-4.2679pt\rangle}
) only if the environment does not offer a write
w
(on the left of
\mathrel{+\hskip-4.2679pt\rangle}
) at the same time, concurrently.

Another important study in the endeavour of adding priorities to
CCS
is the work of Phillips
[
33
]
. In Phillips’ extension of
CCS
with priority guards, referred to as
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
, the previous behavior is expressed by

S\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}{\{\,\}{:}w}{\mathsf{.}}\,S^{\prime}+{\{w\}{:}r}{\mathsf{.}}\,S^{\prime\prime}
,
where each action is pre-annotated by a set of actions that take priority. Process
S
will synchronise with the reader process
R
to move to the error state
S^{\prime\prime}
with
guarded
action
\{w\}{:}r
. The
priority guard

\{w\}
states that this can only happen if the environment does not offer a write
w
. Otherwise, the only action available is
\{\,\}{:}w
which makes the cell move into state
S^{\prime}
. The write action has an empty guard and so cannot be blocked.
If both
\overline{w}
and
\overline{r}
are offered concurrently, then only the higher-prioritised write action
\overline{w}
will go ahead.

The standard way to implement this scheduling in prioritised
CCS
is via a transition relation that keeps track of blocking information. In line with the approaches of
[
10
,
33
,
36
]
, let us write

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}
to denote that process
P
can engage in an action
\alpha
to continue as
P^{\prime}
, provided the concurrent environment does not offer any action from
H
.
We will call
H
the
blocking set
of the transition.
In Plotkin’s SOS, the (selected) rules for prefix and choice in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
are the following

\alpha

P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P_{1}^{\prime}

P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}P_{2}^{\prime}

P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}}}\,$}P_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,P_{2}^{\prime}

More in details, rules

for

\alpha_{i}

H_{i}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{3-i})=\{\,\}
is satisfied.
Rule

in

if it does not offer any action that blocks action
\alpha_{i}
.

A.2
Asynchronous
CCS
with (Strong) Priorities is Confluent

Our example from the previous section shows how (weak) priority-based scheduling helps us to exercise control over the execution order in an asynchronous process calculus with rendez-vous communication.
The surprising observation is that very little needs to be changed in the traditional notion of priority for
CCS
to achieve confluence. For this we need to look how to “tune” of the classical weak priorities for our purposes. Our fix will make the difference between
(weak) priority-based
and
(strong) priority-based
scheduling (see Def.
3
).
What we want is that every process using only strong priority satisfies the following
weak local confluence
property
[
21
]
. Suppose
P
admits two transitions to different states
P_{1}\neq P_{2}
that do not block each other, i.e.,

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P_{1}\text{ and }P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}P_{2}
such that
\alpha_{1}\not\in H_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}\not\in H_{1}
.
Then, each action
\alpha_{i}
(for
i\in\{1,2\}
) is still admissible in
P_{3-i}
, and there exist
P^{\prime}
and
H_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{i}
such that

P_{i}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{3-i}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{i}^{\prime}}}\,$}P^{\prime}.
The assumption
\alpha_{i}\not\in H_{3-i}
uses the blocking sets to express non-interference between transitions.
Then, our weak
5
5

5

In the pure
\lambda
-calculus the diamond property holds for
\beta
-reduction without any assumptions. We use the term “weak” confluence, because of the side-condition of non-interference.
local confluence expresses a weak “diamond property” stating that every diverging choice of non-interfering transitions immediately reconverges. The subset inclusions
H_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{i}
ensure that delaying an admissible action does not introduce more chances of interference.

A.2.1
Fix 1

The standard notion of priority for
CCS
, as implemented in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
or
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
, of course was not designed to restrict
CCS
for determinism but to extend it conservatively to be able to express scheduling control.
Even in the fragment of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
where all choices are prioritised, weak confluence fails.
For instance, although our memory cell
S
behaves deterministically for a single reader
R
and a single writer
W
, in the context
E\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}W_{1}\,|\,[\cdot]\,|\,W_{2}
of two writers
W_{i}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}W_{i}^{\prime}
, it creates a race.
The two writers compete for the single write action on
S
and only one can win.
Using the operational semantics of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
we can derive the (non-interfering) transitions

W_{1}\,|\,S\,|\,W_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\,\}}}\,$}W_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,S^{\prime}\,|\,W_{2}\text{ and }W_{1}\,|\,S\,|\,W_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\,\}}}\,$}W_{1}\,|\,S^{\prime}\,|\,W_{2}^{\prime}.
Still, the two outcomes of the divergence are a critical pair that have no reason to reconverge
W_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,S^{\prime}\,|\,W_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}}\!\!{}\,$}P^{\prime}
and
W_{1}\,|\,S^{\prime}\,|\,W_{2}^{\prime}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}}\!\!{}\,$}P^{\prime}
if
W_{i}^{\prime}
are arbitrary behaviours.
To preserve confluence we must block the environment from consuming the single prefix
w{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}
twice.
The problem is that the synchronisations

W_{1}\,|\,S\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}W_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,S^{\prime}\text{ and }S\,|\,W_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}S\,|\,W_{2}^{\prime}
have empty blocking sets
H_{i}=\{\,\}
which does not prevent the second writer
W_{3-i}
to be added in parallel. If
w\in H_{i}
, then the rules
(\textit{Par}_{3-i})
would automatically prevent the second reader.
Now, suppose we extend the action rule
(\textit{Act})
to expose the singleton nature of a prefix, like
by
(\textit{Act}^{+})
,
\alpha{\mathsf{.}}P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\alpha\}}}\,$}P

we would not only prevent a second concurrent writer but already a single writer. The transitions

W_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{w}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{w}\}}}\,$}W_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }S\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{w}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w\}}}\,$}S^{\prime}
would not be able to communicate, violating both side-condition

\{\overline{w}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(S)=\{\,\}
and
\{w\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(W_{1})=\{\,\}
of
(\textit{Par}_{3})
.
The issue is that in the setting of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
(and
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
, indeed) the blocking set
H
of a transition

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}
expresses a priority rather than an interference. An action cannot take priority over itself without blocking itself. Therefore, in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
(and
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
) we always have
\alpha\not\in H
.
Here, we need to apply a weaker interpretation.
The actions in
H
only prevent
\alpha
from synchronising with a matching
\overline{\alpha}
in the environment, if they are executed in a
different
(and thus competing) prefix from the one that executes
\overline{\alpha}
.
Actions in
H

interfere
with
\alpha
in the sense that they are intial actions of
P
sharing the same control thread in which
\alpha
is offered.
Thus, we want to avoid a situation in which the environment synchronises with
\overline{\alpha}
in the presence of
another distinct
communication on some
\beta\in H
.
For then we would have a race for the behaviour of the thread shared by
\alpha
and
\beta
in
P
.

To fix this, we need to refine the side-conditions
H_{i}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{3-i})
in
(\textit{Par}_{3})
to check not
all
initial actions
\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{3-i})
but only those initial actions of
P_{3-i}
that are
competing
with the matching transitions

P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}P_{2}^{\prime}
with
\alpha_{1}=\overline{\alpha}_{2}
, involved in the communication
P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}}}\,$}P_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,P_{2}^{\prime}
that we want to protect. We fix this by refining the information exposed by a transition. More specifically, we extend each transition

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{K}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}
by the set
K
of initial actions that are offered in competing prefixes of the same thread as
\alpha
or by threads concurrent to the thread from which
\alpha
is taken.
Then, the set of all initial actions is simply

\textsf{iA}(P)=K\cup\{\alpha\}
.
Let us call
K
the
initial environment
of the transition. From the description, we expect the invariant that
H\subseteq K
.

Remark 27
.

Note that we cannot simply define
K\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\textsf{iA}(P)-\{\alpha\}
, because
\alpha
could be contained in
K
. For example, take
P\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\alpha{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\alpha{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
, where

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{\alpha\}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\alpha\}}}\,$}\alpha{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}.

The blocking set is
H=\{\alpha\}
since the prefix is volatile and must be protected from a competing consumptions.
Also, we need
\alpha
to be in the initial environment
K=\{\alpha\}
, because the transition’s action
\alpha
is offered a second time in a concurrent thread. This is different from the set
\textsf{iA}(P)-\{\alpha\}=\{\alpha\}-\{\alpha\}=\{\,\}
. This shows that we must compute the initial environment explicitly as an extra label with each transition.

Now we can reformulate the parallel rule as

P_{i}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{K_{i}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{i}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{i}}}\,$}P_{i}^{\prime}

\alpha_{1}=\overline{\alpha}_{2}

H_{i}\cap\overline{K}_{3-i}=\{\,\}

for
i\in\{1,2\}

(\textit{Par}_{3}^{+})

P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{K_{1}\cup K_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}}}\,$}P_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,P_{2}^{\prime}

and achieve the same as before but can accommodate the action rule
(\textit{Act}^{+})
that introduces a “reflexive precedence”.

A.2.2
Fix 2

Figure 6:
The store
S
in composition with a reader
\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
and a delayed writer
d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{d}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
.
The behaviour has two incongruent final outcomes, a store
S^{\prime\prime}
with error in state ⑤ and an error-free store
S
in state ⑥. The transition system is not confluent.
The reason is that transition labelled
\tau{:}\{w\}
is admissible in state ① but not anymore in state ②. A solution is to block the reading already in state ①. In other words, the priority blocking (write-before-read) that kicks in at state ② needs to be detected already at state ①. Again, all transitons are silent with labels omitted.

In the previous subsection we have seen how priorities can be beneficial to approaching to a deterministic scheduling in an intrinsic non-deterministic process algebra.
The priority blocking eliminates the competition between mutually pre-empting actions and the remaining independent actions naturally commute.
Now another question naturally arises: “does this mean that if we replace the non-deterministic choice
P+Q
by a prioritised choice, denoted by
P\mathrel{+\hskip-4.2679pt\rangle}Q
, do we obtain a determinate process algebra?”
Indeed, we can show that in the “asynchronous” fragment of
CCS
, i.e. where all prefixes
\alpha{\mathsf{.}}P
are of form
\alpha{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
, confluence can be granted:
On the contrary, in the “synchronous” fragment of
CCS
, where actions sequentially guard new actions, we run into a problem of non-monotonicity. A communication between prefixes may trigger new actions that block another communication that was enabled before. In other words, it is not guaranteed that independent actions remain admissible.
As an example, in the above reader-writer scenario, the actions
a_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) may become blocked in processes
P_{3-i}
. Now, consider a parallel composition
R\,|\,S^{*}\,|\,W^{*}
in which a modified store
S^{*}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
offers another concurrent method action
d
on the side in parallel. Let the writer
W^{*}
be delayed by a synchronisation with this concurrent action
d
, i.e.,
W^{*}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{d}{\mathsf{.}}W\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{d}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}.

The scheduling sequences are depicted in Fig.
6
.
We can still derive

S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{w,d\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{r}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{r,w\}}}\,$}S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\text{ but also }S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{r,w\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{d}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{d\}}}\,$}S.
The reading synchronisation

R\,|\,S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{w,d\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{r},r,w\}}}\,$}S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
can now take place by
(\textit{Par}_{3})
before the write to give the following reduction sequence:

R\,|\,S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,W^{*}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{w,d,\overline{d}\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{r},r,w\}}}\,$}S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,W^{*}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{r,e\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{d\}}}\,$}S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,W.
The first reduction is justified by rule
(\textit{Par}_{1})
, because the side-condition
\{w\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(W)=\{w\}\cap\{d\}=\{\,\}
is satisfied. The blocking side-condition which only looks at the moment when the read is happening does not see the write action
\overline{w}
by
W^{*}
hidden behind its initial action
\overline{d}
. It lets process
R
access and read the store
S
.
As before, however, the following reduction sequence is allowed, too:

R\,|\,S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,W^{*}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{\overline{r},r,w\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{d\}}}\,$}R\,|\,S\,|\,W\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{\overline{r},r\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w,\overline{w}\}}}\,$}R\,|\,S^{\prime}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\{w\}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{r},r,w\}}}\,$}S^{\prime}
in which the write action happens before the read action.
This creates two diverging reduction sequences (incoherent schedules) in which the final store is
S^{\prime}
or
S^{\prime\prime}
, non-deterministically.

Our insight is that the blocking side-conditions of the standard Camilleri-Winskell theory
[
10
]
, described above, are too weak in scheduling control for our purposes and to guaranteeing a weak Church-Rosser property
[
21
]
.
The problem is that in the side-condition of rule
\textit{Par}_{2}
we are only looking at the initial actions of
Q
. This is why the read action is blocked out of state ② in Fig.
6
but not out of state ①. In state ① there is no pending initial write. The write action
\overline{w}
only becomes initial when the parallel processes
\overline{d}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
have interacted on action
d
and advanced together with a silent action
\tau
, i.e.
\overline{d}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
. In order to see that the read action
r
blocked by
\{w\}
out of state ① should not be enabled, we must follow all sequences of interactions that can possibly happen concurrently to the read. The read is to be blocked, because after one silent
\tau
-step, in the reachable configuration of state ②, the write action
w
in the blocking set
\{w\}
of a silent action, meets a matching initial partner
\overline{w}
.

This suggests a strategy to fix up the standard theory. We accumulate, along with the
upper
blocking set
H
of a transition the following: a
lower
blocking set
L
and the context of all concurrent processes
E
that run in competition with the transition. From these we can find out what blocking actions might be generated after any number of silent actions.
The initial environment
K
of a transition
then is
H\cup L\cup\textsf{iA}(E)
.
This finally gives a transition relation of the form

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{H\cup L}}\,$}P^{\prime}.
The label
E
, called the
reduction context
, is accumulated in the parallel compositions of
P
.
The rules
(\textit{Act})
and
(\textit{PriSum}_{i})
are the one presented above.
Further, let
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(E)
be informally the set of all actions that can become initial along
\tau
-sequences from
E
, which obviously includes
\textsf{iA}(E)
(formally defined later on). We will call the set
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(E)
the set of
weak initial
actions of
E
.
The rules
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{1})
and
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{2})
need to be reformulated. Rule
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{1})
, propagating an action in a parallel composition, is as follows:

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{H\cup L}}\,$}P^{\prime}

E^{\prime}=E\,|\,Q

H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}^{\tau}}(E^{\prime})=\{\,\}

(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{1})

P\,|\,Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{H\cup L}}\,$}P^{\prime}\,|\,Q

Rule
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{2})
is symmetric to
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{1})
.
Rule
(\textit{Par}_{3})
, for the synchronisation of an action
\alpha
and its co-action
\overline{\alpha}
using these weak initial sets, is reformulated as follows:

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}\cup L_{1}}}\,$}P^{\prime}

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\alpha}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}\cup L_{2}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}

E=E_{1}\,|\,E_{2}

\forall i.\,H_{i}\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}^{\tau}}(E)\cup L_{3-i})=\{\,\}

(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{3})

P\,|\,Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}\cup L_{1}\cup L_{2}}}\,$}P^{\prime}\,|\,Q^{\prime}

We are now ready to revisit our running example.
By
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{2})
, since

R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{r}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{r\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}R^{\prime}\text{ and }S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{r}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w,r\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}S^{\prime\prime}\text{, we get }R\,|\,S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{w,r\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}.

The side-condition of
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{3})
is satisfied since the “weak” initial action set (where
\tau
actions are dropped)
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}})=\{d\}
.
Thus, both
\{r\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}})=\{\,\}
as well as
\{w,r\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}})=\{\,\}
.
If now want to shunt this past the writer process
W^{*}
using rule
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{1})
, then we get stuck.
Since

d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,W^{*}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{d,\overline{d}\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}W
and
\overline{w}\in\textsf{iA}(W)
, we find
\overline{w}\in\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,W^{*})

and thus
\{w\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,W^{*})\neq\{\,\}
. Therefore, the side-condition of
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{1})
is violated and the reading interaction of
R\,|\,S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
cannot be composed with
W^{*}
.
This is what we want. The transition from state ② to state ③ in Fig.
6
is now blocked.
On the other hand, the reduction

R\,|\,S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,|\,S}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{d}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{d\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}R\,|\,S\text{ can be composed with }W^{*}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{d}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{d}\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}W

via
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{3})
to make a reduction

R\,|\,S\,|\,d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,W^{*}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,|\,S}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{d,\overline{d}\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}R\,|\,S\,|\,W.
Regarding the side-condition, we notice that the weak initial action set
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(R\,|\,S)
of the reduction context is irrelevant, because it does not contain
d
or
\overline{d}
.
In configuration
R\,|\,S\,|\,W
(state ② in Fig.
6
), the reader
R
cannot interact with
S
. The associated reduction

R\,|\,S\,|\,W\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{W}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{r,w\}\cup\{\,\}}}\,$}S^{\prime\prime}\,|\,W
is blocked by the reduction environment, because

w\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}W^{\prime})
.
For our further discussion, let us call the proposed change in the side-conditions leading to
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{i})
from
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{i})
a process algebra with
strong priorities
6
6

6

The terminology is suggested by the fact that strong priorities use weak initial sets as rule premises while weak priorities are based on strong initial sets. Rules are implications, so weakening the antecedent makes a rule logically stronger.
and let’s call the traditional process algebra with priorities, introduced by
[
10
,
36
]
, as process algebra with
weak priorities
.

As an intermediate step to implement our precedence-based scheduling, the previous example suggests we look at the
weak initial actions
for a process, given in the following definition.

Definition 28

(Weak Initial Actions & Strong Enabling)
.

The set
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)
of weak initial transitions is the smallest extension
\textsf{iA}(P)\subseteq\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)
such that if
\alpha\in\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(Q)
and
P\xrightarrow{\tau}Q

then
\alpha\in\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)
.

Let
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(P)=\overline{\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)}
.
A transition

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}

is called strongly enabled if
H\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(R)\cup\{\tau\})=\{\,\}
.

It is easy to see that
\textsf{iA}(P)\subseteq\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)
, so every strongly enabled transition is weakly enabled: as such, for free processes, the notions of weak and strong enabling coincide. Note also that enabling as a scheduling constraint is not monotonic under parallel composition. The enabledness property of a transition is not, in general, preserved when the process is placed into a concurrent context. If a reduction

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}
is enabled, then we know that
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R)=\{\,\}
or
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(R)=\{\,\}
, depending on what scheduling we choose. This means that the context
R
cannot (weakly) generate a synchronisation with a blocking initial action. This does not mean that an extension
R\,|\,Q
may not perhaps have
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R\,|\,Q)\neq\{\,\}
or
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(R\,|\,Q)\neq\{\,\}
. This happens if blocking actions come from
Q
or, in the case of strong enabling, become reachable though the interactions of
R
and
Q
. Then the parallel extension
P\,|\,Q
does not permit a reduction

P\,|\,Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\,|\,Q.
As indicated by the examples of Sec.
3
, strong enabling generates confluent reductions in the reflexive case, where rendez-vous actions have a unique sender and a unique receiver process. For multi-cast communication, i.e., non-irreflexive self-blocking c-actions, we need the even stronger notion of
constructive enabling
based on
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R)
instead of
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(R)
.

A.2.3
Digression: Strong Priorities lead to Impredicativity Issues

On the face of it, strong priorities may seem rather a natural modification to make. However, they amount to a significant change of the game compared to the standard weak priorities presented in the literature. For they break the standard method of defining the transition relation inductively as the least relation closed under a finite set of production rules.
The key problem is the logical cycle in the structural definition of the operational semantics: the rules to generate the transitions
ensure
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(E)=\{\,\}
, i.e., that none of the blocking actions
H
matches with the transitively reachable initial actions of the reduction context
E
which itself is extracted as a part of
P
.

Obviously, to compute
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(E)
we need to know the full transition relation for
E
. In other words, the existence of a transition of a process
P
depends on the absence of certain transitions of a certain part of process
P
. If
P
is defined recursively or via repetition then the behaviour of a part of
P
might depend on the behaviour of
P
itself. Does such a self-referential semantical definition make sense at all? How do we ensure the absence of a Russell-paradox style relation, by defining a process
\mathop{\textsf{rec}}p.\,P
to have a transition to
P^{\prime}
if it does not have a transition to
P^{\prime}
for a suitable
P^{\prime}
? Such would be plainly inconsistent at the meta-level,
because it would not only define simply an empty transition relation
7
7

7

As an example consider the unguarded process
\mathop{\textsf{rec}}p{\mathsf{.}}p
in the standard inductive theory. Its behaviour is well-defined as the empty transition relation.
,
but it would not define a unique semantic relation at all.

In the standard theory of weakly prioritised process algebra as described in the cited references of
CCS
with priorities, the problem does not exist, because the set of (strong) initial actions
\textsf{iA}(E)
can be determined by structural recursion on
E
without any reference to the transition relation. Unfortunately, for strong prioritized process algebra as defined in this paper, this is not possible anymore.
So, how do we make sense of transition-generating rules
(\textit{Par}^{{}_{\ast}}_{i})
that depend on negative premises? One option is to treat the negation in the side-condition
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(E)=\{\,\}
constructively rather than classically, say like negation in normal logic programming. This would amount to building a second independent (inductive) rule system to generate constructive judgements about the absence of weakly reachable transitions. Trouble is that there is no canonical semantics of constructive negation, even in logic programming
8
8

8

Different well-known semantics are negation-by-failure, stable semantics, supported model semantics.
.

Some progress has been made for unifying the proposals in a game-theoretic and intuitionistic setting which works well for special situations like the constructive semantics of Esterel
[
5
]
or StateCharts
[
17
]
. However, playing with constructive negation at the meta-level will give a variety of semantics whose adequacy might be difficult to judge in practical application contexts.

A more perspicuous attack is to keep the classical set-theoretic interpretation
but use an over-approximation
L\cap\overline{\textsf{fiA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)=\{\,\}
where
\textsf{fiA}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)\supseteq\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(E)
is intuitively a superset of weakly reachable initial sender actions obtained from a relaxed transition relation, formally defined later in this paper. A natural over-approximation that can be defined independently is the “free speculative” scheduling that ignores all priorities. The stronger side-condition
L\cap\overline{\textsf{fiA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)=\{\,\}
forces the absence of a blocking sender action under the worst-case assumption of priority-free scheduling. Obviously, this is sound but will have the effect that some programs block that could make progress under a tighter approximation of the sets
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(E)
. Between
\textsf{fiA}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)
and
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(E)
there be a whole range of different semantics that may or may not be equivalent to specific constructive semantics of negation. Exploring the range of options systematically will certainly be a worthwhile research exercise likely to cover much new ground in the theory of process algebras with priorities.

A.3
Synchronous
CCS
with (Strong) Priorities and Clocks is Confluent

We argue that the use of priorities for determinacy calls for a combination of strong priorities with a second important concept that has been investigated in the literature before, but independently. This is the well-known notion of a
clock
, from Timed Process Algebras, like e.g.
TPL

[
18
]
and
PMC

[
15
]
.
Intuitively, a clock
\sigma
is a “broadcast action” in the spirit of Hoare’s
CSP

[
19
]
that acts as a synchronisation barrier for a set of concurrent processes. It bundles each processes’ actions into a sequence of
macro-steps
that are aligned with each other in a lock-step fashion. During each macro-step, a process executes only a temporal slice of its total behaviour, at the end of which it waits for all other processes in the same
clock scope
to reach the end of the current phase. When all processes have reached the barrier, they are released into the next round of computation.

This scheduling principle is well-known from computer hardware like sequential circuits
[
39
]
and VHDL
[
20
]
, or synchronous languages like Esterel
[
5
]
, Lustre
[
16
]
, SCCharts
[
41
]
, BSP
[
40
]
, just to mention a few. For our purposes, clocks are the universal trick to break causality cycles in priority scheduling, when processes need to communicate with each other in iterated cycles (so-called
ticks
or
macro-steps
) while maintaining determinacy.

To get this off the ground we introduce a clock broadcast action
\sigma
to separate each round of communication. We define the recursive processes

W\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}d{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{d}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w}{\mathsf{.}}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}W

and
R\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{r}{\mathsf{.}}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}R

for one write and one read per macro-step. Assuming the content of the memory variable is reset for every tick, we can define
S
as follows

S\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}w{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}+r{:}\{w\}{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}+\sigma{:}\{w,r\}{\mathsf{.}}S
with
S^{\prime}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}r{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}+\sigma{:}\{r\}{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime}

and
S^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}w{\mathsf{.}}\text{fail}+r{:}\{w\}{\mathsf{.}}S^{\prime\prime}+\sigma{:}\{w,r\}{\mathsf{.}}S
.
The
clock always takes
lowest
priority among all other rendez-vous actions out of a state.
The idea is that the clock should fire only if the system has stabilised and no other admissible data actions are possible. This is called
maximal progress
and it is a standard assumption in timed process algebras
[
18
,
11
]
.
The fairly standard rules for clock actions, inspired by timed process algebras are then:

(\textit{Clk}_{1})

\sigma{\mathsf{.}}P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\,\}}}\,$}P

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{L_{1}}}\,$}P^{\prime}

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{L_{2}}}\,$}P^{\prime}

(L_{1}\cup L_{2})\cap\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(R_{1}\,|\,R_{2})=\{\,\}

(\textit{Clk}_{2})

P\,|\,Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}\,|\,R_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{L_{1}\cup L_{2}}}\,$}P^{\prime}\,|\,Q^{\prime}

Those two rules enforces global synchronisation and make the clock deterministic.
As such, the evaluation of
R\,|\,S\,|\,W
“prunes” all reductions that could lead to non confluent reductions.

Figure 7:
Strong priorities introduce confluence in scheduling.

Fig.
7
shows how higher priorities can introduce determinism in scheduling.

The importance of the clock
\sigma
is that its associated synchronisation rule
(\textit{Clk}_{2})
enforces, in a quite natural way, the barrier explicitly in the SOS rather than making it an artefact of a specific synchronisation process, as such obliging the programmer to an extra effort. The fact that the broadcasting clock is hard-wired into the rules can now be exploited for the computation of the
weak initial action sets
, defined formally
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(R)
.
More precisely, we only need to predict the behaviour of
R
for the current macro-step, i.e., up to the next synchronization of
\sigma
by
R
. For a transition
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{L}}\,$}P^{\prime}
we know that all actions taking place after
\sigma
in
R
are definitely observed to happen in the next macro step and thus not in competition with the current action
a
under blocking set
L
. In this fashion, the clock acts as as a
stopper sentinel
for prediction and evaluation of the blocking set
L
relative to the reduction environment
R
. If every recursion is instantaneous, then the search space becomes finite.

As a final remark, for the purposes of this paper, adding synchrony and determinism in process algebras, we are not (and indeed we do not need to be) interested in higher-order calculi, like e.g. the
\pi
-calculus
[
29
,
30
]
, where processes are
first-class
objects, i.e. sent and received as a values, as in
a{!}P{\mathsf{.}}Q
and
a{?}x{\mathsf{.}}(x\,|\,Q)
.
In the next sections, we present the syntax, the structural operational semantics à la Plotkin, we set up all the definitions and the metatheory necessary to formally prove the claimed informal properties.

Appendix B
Examples, new and reloaded

This section contains a number of reloaded and new examples in view of the definitions and results presented in the previous section.

Note on Conservativity

The weakly-enabled transitions of unclocked, free processes coincide with the semantics of genuine Milner’s
CCS

[
27
]
, as stated in the following easy exercise.

Exercise B.29

(On Def.
2
)
.

If
P
is unclocked and free, then a transition

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q

is weakly enabled iff
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q
is admissible, i.e., iff it is derivable in Milner’s original SOS for
CCS
.

In fact, one can show that the semantics of weak enabling generates the theory of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}

[
33
]
.
To be more precise, let us call a process
P

irreflexive
if no action prefix
\alpha{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
occurring in
P
has
\alpha\in H
, i.e., blocks itself. In the semantics of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
, such
self-blocking
prefixes do not contribute any transitions. Thus,
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
captures a theory of irreflexive processes. We leave as a less easy exercise that all weakly enabled transitions between irreflexive processes are precisely the ones allowed in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
.

Exercise B.30

(On Def.
2
)
.

If
P
is unclocked and irreflexive, then a transition

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q

is weakly enabled iff
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}
is derivable in the operational semantics of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
.

Thus, from the point of view of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, theories of
CCS
and
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{Ph}}
correspond to theories of free and irreflexive processes, respectively, under weak enabling. Here we suggest two extension to this classical work. Firstly, we apply a stronger scheduling restrictions, called
strong enabling
(Def.
28
below) and
constructive enabling
(Def.
3
above) to achieve confluence and secondly we make use of self-loops to control sharing.

Priorities and Scheduling

Example B.31

(On Def.
12
)
.

If
A\not\equiv B
then
a{\mathsf{.}}A+a{\mathsf{.}}B
is not coherent while
a{:}a{\mathsf{.}}A\,|\,a{:}a{\mathsf{.}}B
is coherent.
Conflicting choices on the same label in a single thread cannot be externally observed and thus cannot be deterministically scheduled via their externally observable properties. In coherent processes such choices are assumed to be resolved internally by each thread itself, rather than through the precedence relation.
Note that the process
a{:}a{\mathsf{.}}A+a{:}a{\mathsf{.}}B
in which the two action prefixes are self-blocking, is not coherent either. The reason is that the two identical (self-blocking) transitions lead to structurally distinct states
A\not\equiv B
. Thus, the confluence condition expressed in Def.
12
applies and
A
and
B
should be reconvergent with transitions
A\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q^{\prime}
and
B\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q^{\prime}
. But this is not guaranteed for arbitrary
A
and
B
. On the other hand, a single self-blocking prefix
a{:}a{\mathsf{.}}A
is coherent. Without the self-blocking,
a{\mathsf{.}}A
is only coherent if process
A
permits infinite repetition of
a
as in
A\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a{\mathsf{.}}A
or
A\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a\,|\,A
.

The precedence relation cannot resolve the choice of a prefix with itself. The precedence relation can resolve the choice between distinct actions prefixes. For instance, if
a\neq b
then
a{\mathsf{.}}A+b{\mathsf{.}}B
is not coherent, for any
A
and
B
. However, both
a{:}a{\mathsf{.}}A\,|\,b{:}b{\mathsf{.}}B
and
a{:}a{\mathsf{.}}A+b{:}\{a,b\}{\mathsf{.}}B
are coherent. In the former case, the actions are concurrently independent and so confluence occurs naturally. In the latter case, the choice is resolved by precedence: No reconvergence is required by coherence Def.
12
as the actions are interfering with each other. ∎

Example B.32

(On Normal form)
.

For the process
P_{1}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}(a{\mathsf{.}}c\,|\,b{\mathsf{.}}d\,|\,\overline{a}\,|\,\overline{b})\,\backslash\,\{a,b\}
we have

(a{\mathsf{.}}c\,|\,d\,|\,\overline{a})\,\backslash\,\{a,b\}\xleftarrow[\raisebox{-1.68887pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\tau}\!\!{}\,P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{}\,$}(c\,|\,b{\mathsf{.}}d\,|\,\overline{b})\,\backslash\,\{a,b\}
where the continuation processes
(c\,|\,b{\mathsf{.}}d\,|\,\overline{b})\,\backslash\,\{a,b\}
and
(a{\mathsf{.}}c\,|\,d\,|\,\overline{a})\,\backslash\,\{a,b\}
are not structurally equivalent. They are not even behaviourally equivalent.
However, both reductions are concurrently independent and commute with each other. We can execute both rendezvous synchronisations
\tau=a\,|\,\overline{a}
and
\tau=b\,|\,\overline{b}
in any order, the process
P
converges to a unique final outcome
(c\,|\,d)\,\backslash\,\{a,b\}
. Similarly, in the multi-cast process
P_{2}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}(a{\mathsf{.}}c\,|\,a{\mathsf{.}}d\,|\,\overline{a}\,|\,\overline{a})\,\backslash\,\{a\}
the reduction behaviour is not structurally deterministic but nevertheless generates a unique normal form.
∎

Example B.33

(On Sequentiality à la Esterel in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
)
.

Like
CCS
, our calculus
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
does not have a generic operator for sequential composition as in Esterel. This is not necessary, because sequential composition can be coded in
CCS
by action prefixing and parallel composition. More precisely, a sequential composition
P;Q
is obtained as a parallel composition
([P]_{t}\,|\,t{:}t{\mathsf{.}}Q)\,\backslash\,\{t\}
where
[P]_{t}
is the process
P
, modified so when it terminates, then the termination signal
\overline{t}
is sent. The signal
t
will then trigger the sequentially downstream process
Q
. The operation
[P]_{t}
can be implemented by a simple (linear) syntactic transformation of
P
.
In the same way we can detect termination of a parallel composition
P=P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}
. Since a parallel terminates if both threads terminate, we can define
[P]_{t}
as follows

[P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}]_{t}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}([P_{1}]_{s_{1}}\,|\,[P_{2}]_{s_{2}}\,|\,s_{1}{:}s_{1}{\mathsf{.}}s_{2}{:}s_{2}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{t})\,\backslash\,\{s_{1},s_{2}\}

where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are fresh “local” termination signals.
The “termination detector”
T_{\textit{seq}}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}s_{1}{:}s_{1}{\mathsf{.}}s_{2}{:}s_{2}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{t}
first waits for
P_{1}
to terminate and then for
P_{2}
, before it sends the signal
\overline{t}
indicating the termination of the parallel composition. We could equally first wait for
P_{2}
and then for
P_{1}
, if the difference is not observable from the outside.
In cases where the termination detector needs to permit both processes
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
to terminate in any order, however, we need a different solution. We need to “wait concurrently” for
s_{1}
or
s_{1}
, not sequentially as in
T_{\textit{seq}}
. The obvious idea would be to use a confluent sum

T_{\textit{csum}}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}s_{1}{:}s_{1}{\mathsf{.}}s_{2}{:}s_{2}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{t}+s_{2}{:}s_{2}{\mathsf{.}}s_{1}{:}s_{1}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{t}

as discussed in Milner
[
27
]
(Chap. 11.4).
This does not work, because the sum must explicitly unfold all possible orders in which the termination signals can arrive and thus again creates an exponential descriptive complexity.
Interestingly, in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
we can exploit precedence scheduling to obtain a robust and compositional solution. Consider the process

T_{\textit{par}}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}s{\mathsf{.}}T_{\textit{par}}+\overline{t}{:}\{s,\overline{t}\}.
It engages in an unbounded number of receptions on channel
s
without changing its state. At the same time, it offers to send the termination signal
\overline{t}
, but only if the action
s
is not possible. Under the weak enabling strategy, the composition
\overline{s}\,|\,\overline{s}\,|\,T
will consume the two senders
\overline{s}
and only then engage in
t
. In this way, we can use
T_{\textit{par}}
to detect the absence of
s
which corresponds to the termination of the two environment processes
\overline{s}\,|\,\overline{s}
, in any order.
The signal
s
could be the shared termination signal of each process
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
that is sent and consumed by
T_{\textit{par}}
if and when the process terminates.
If we execute the parallel composition
P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}\,|\,T_{\textit{par}}
under constructive enabling, the termination signal
\overline{t}
will not be sent until such time that both
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
have been able to send
s
to
T
.
From the scheduling point of view, the composition
P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}
acts like a counter that is initialised to
2
, representing the number of potential offerings of
s
initially in
P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}
. It gets decremented each time one of
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
terminates. For instance,
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,P_{2}
and
P_{1}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}
have one potential offering left, while
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}
has no more potential to send
s
. At this moment, the counter has reached
{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,T_{\textit{par}}
can take the
\overline{t}
action and thereby send termination.
In sum, there are two important observations here: First, we find that precedences with constructive enabling permits us to code sequential composition of Esterel in a compositional fashion. Second, the coding of Milner’s confluent sum
T_{\textit{csum}}
is possible in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
with linear descriptive complexity. ∎

The next example illustrates that the closure property for coherence of hiding (Thm.
14
(6), see also Prop.
D.84
) needs to require that the hidden clock must not take precedence over rendezvous actions.

Example B.34

(On Clock-hiding)
.

The process
P\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}A+a{:}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}B\,|\,\sigma
offers a choice of two transitions

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\,\}}}\,$}A

and

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\sigma\}}}\,$}B\,|\,\sigma.
These transitions interfere with each other so that the
\sigma
-transition blocks the
a
-transition under weak enabling, given that
\{\sigma\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(\sigma)=\{\sigma\}\neq\{\,\}
. Thus,
P
is coherent.
Note that any policy with
P\Vdash\pi
must have
\sigma\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}a\in\pi
. This does not satisfy the side condition of Thm.
24
(6). So we cannot conclude that
P\,/\penalty\,\sigma
is coherent.
Let us see what happens when the clock is hidden and thus generates a silent step. Indeed, hiding the clock, we obtain from rule
Hide
the two transitions

P\,/\penalty\,\sigma\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,/\penalty\,\sigma}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\,\}}}\,$}A\,/\penalty\,\sigma\text{ and }P\,/\penalty\,\sigma\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma\,/\penalty\,\sigma}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\}}}\,$}(B\,|\,\sigma)\,/\penalty\,\sigma

where the blocking set of the
a
-transition turns into
\{\sigma\}-\{\sigma\}=\{\,\}
.
Now, because
\sigma
is removed from the blocking set, the
a
-transition is even constructively enabled. Moreover, both the
\tau
-transition and the
a
-transition are interference-free. However, in general, there is no chance that we can find recovergent transitions
A\,/\penalty\,\sigma\xrightarrow{a}Q_{1}
and
(B\,|\,\sigma)\,/\penalty\,\sigma\xrightarrow{\tau}Q_{2}
with
Q_{1}\equiv Q_{2}
. Thus,
P\,/\penalty\,\sigma
is not coherent any more. ∎

The problem is this: One the one hand, the
Hide
rule closes the scope of a clock but one the other hand, it does not actually implement any blocking that comes from this clock inside the scope. Such can be implemented by a refined hiding rule that, like
(\textit{Com})
, implements a “race test” and introduces a
\tau
into the blocking set whenever there is a conflict:

P\,/\penalty\,\sigma\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,/\penalty\,\sigma}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha\,/\penalty\,\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}Q\,/\penalty\,\sigma\lx@proof@logical@and P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}QH^{\prime}=H-\{\sigma\}\cup\{\tau\mid\sigma\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R)\cap H\}

In this manner, the enabling check for the conclusion transition verifies that
\tau\not\in H^{\prime}
and thus that
\sigma\not\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R)\cap H
. In other words, if the local clock
\sigma
is in
H
(blocking the visible action
\alpha
) then the concurrent context
R
inside the clock scope must not conflict with
\sigma
. Not surprisingly,
mutatis mutandis
, a similar refinement could be made to the restriction rule giving

P\,\backslash\,\ell\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,\backslash\,\ell}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}Q\,\backslash\,\ell\lx@proof@logical@and P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q\alpha\not\in\{\ell,\overline{\ell}\}H^{\prime}=H-\{\ell,\overline{\ell}\}\cup\{\tau\mid\ell\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R)\cap H\}

In this paper we stick to the simpler versions
(\textit{Hide})
and
(\textit{Restr})
but conjecture that the side conditions on the policy in Thm.
24
(6,7) can be lifted to show that for
(\textit{Hide}^{*})
and
(\textit{Restr}^{*})
the operations of hiding and restriction preserve coherence.

Example B.35

(Clock-hiding with built-in Scheduling Test)
.

Consider the process
P\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}A+a{:}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}B\,|\,b{\mathsf{.}}\sigma
where the local clock
\sigma
also blocks the action
a
by priority. But now the clock is guarded in
b{\mathsf{.}}\sigma
and so to become active for
P
, it needs the synchronisation with an external action
\overline{b}
.
As in the previous example
P\,/\penalty\,\sigma
would not be coherent.
The extended hiding rule
(\textit{Hide}^{*})
would generate the
a
-transition

P\,/\penalty\,\sigma\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{(b{\mathsf{.}}\sigma)\,/\penalty\,\sigma}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\tau\}}}\,$}(B\,|\,b{\mathsf{.}}\sigma)\,/\penalty\,\sigma\text{ from }P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{b{\mathsf{.}}\sigma}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\sigma\}}}\,$}A\,|\,b{\mathsf{.}}\sigma.

The
\tau
enters into the blocking set because of the race test which discovers that

\sigma\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(b{\mathsf{.}}\sigma)\cap\{\sigma\}
, where
\{\sigma\}
is the blocking set of the
a
-transition in
P
. Hence, the
a
-transition of
P\,/\penalty\,\sigma
is not (constructively) enabled anymore, which saves coherence. There is no requirement to exhibit any reconvergence for it. ∎

Policies

Example B.36

(On coherence and pivotable processes)
.

The process
P_{1}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a\,|\,\overline{b}\,|\,\overline{a}{:}b+b
is coherent and pivotable with a policy
\pi
such that
\pi\Vdash b\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{a}
and
\pi\Vdash\overline{b}\mathrel{\diamond}a
. The rendezvous synchronisation
\tau=a\,|\,\overline{a}

is blocked by the presence of the offering
\overline{b}
from a third thread.
We have

P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{b}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{b\}}}\,$}\overline{b},
which is not weakly enabled, because
\{b\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(\overline{b})\neq\{\,\}
. The blocking is due to the concurrent context
\overline{b}
of the transition.
For contrast, the process
P_{2}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}(a+\overline{b})\,|\,(\overline{a}{:}b+b)
is pivotable but not coherent.
In our semantics, the synchronisation of
a
and
\overline{a}{:}b
in
P_{2}
is blocked by the race test of rule
(\textit{Com})
which introduces
\tau
into the blocking set

P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{b,\tau\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}
because
\{b\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(a+\overline{b})\not\subseteq\{\overline{a}\}
. This transition therefore is not weakly enabled, because of
\{b,\tau\}\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}({{\mathsf{0}}})\cup\{\tau\})\neq\{\,\}
. We can make the pivotable
P_{1}
also coherent by forcing a sequential ordering between
a
and
\overline{b}
in the first thread, say
P_{3}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a{\mathsf{.}}\overline{b}\,|\,(\overline{a}{:}b+b)
, then the synchronisation is even strongly enabled

P_{3}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{b\}}}\,$}\overline{b}.
If we introduce a precedence into the first thread, say
P_{4}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}(a{:}b+\overline{b})\,|\,(\overline{a}{:}b+b)
, then we are also coherent but no longer pivotable. Like for
P_{2}
, the synchronisation
\tau=a\,|\,\overline{a}
is blocked and not weakly enabled, because of the introduction of
\tau
into the blocking set by the race condition of
(\textit{Com})
. ∎

Example B.37

(On Coherent and Pivotable Processes)
.

The process
P_{1}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\sigma+a{:}\sigma\,|\,\overline{a}
is coherent and pivotable, with policy
\sigma\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}a\in\pi
and
\sigma\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{a}\in\pi
. It has both
a,\overline{a}\in\textsf{iA}(P)
but
\sigma\not\in\textsf{iA}(P)
since the clock is not offered by the parallel process
\overline{a}
. The synchronisation
\tau=a\,|\,\overline{a}
can go ahead.
On the other hand,
the process
P_{2}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\sigma+a{:}\sigma\,|\,\overline{a}+\sigma{:}\overline{a}
has all three actions
\sigma,a,\overline{a}\in\textsf{iA}(P_{2})
initial. It is coherent but not pivotable. Since both
a
and
\overline{a}
are in a precedence relationship with
\sigma
,
P_{2}
does not satisfy concurrent independence of
\sigma
with neither
a
nor
\overline{a}
. Formally, any policy
\pi
for
P_{2}
must have
\sigma\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}a\in\pi
and
\overline{a}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\sigma\in\pi
violating the pivot requirement that if
\sigma\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}a\in\pi
, then
\sigma\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{a}\in\pi
. The same is true of the process
P_{3}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\sigma+(a{:}\sigma\,|\,\overline{a})
which can perform both
\sigma
and a (silent) reduction
\tau=\alpha\,|\,\overline{\alpha}
that has
\sigma
in its blocking set. ∎

An interesting special class of pivot policies are input-scheduled policies.

Definition B.38

(Input-scheduled Processes)
.

A policy
\pi
is called
input-scheduled if
\pi\subseteq\pi_{is}
where
\pi_{is}=(L_{is},\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{is}$})
given by alphabet
L_{is}=\mathcal{L}
and precedence relation
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi_{is}
iff
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
or
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{C}
.
A process is input-scheduled if
P
conforms to an input-scheduled policy.

Example B.39

(On Pivotable but not Input-scheduled Processes)
.

The process
a+b{:}c\,|\,\overline{a}+\overline{b}{:}\overline{a}
is pivotable. It conforms to
\pi
with precedences
c\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}b\in\pi
and
\overline{a}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{b}\in\pi
, while at the same time
\overline{c}\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{b}\in\pi
and
a\mathrel{\diamond}b\in\pi
. However, assuming
a\neq b
, the process is not input-scheduled, because it has
\overline{a}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{b}\in\pi
while
\overline{a}\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{b}\in\pi_{is}
. The same is true of the process in Ex.
5
. ∎

{subfigure}

Figure 8:
A Boolean Read-Write Memory with coherent states
\mathsf{wAND}_{0}:\mathsf{mem}
and
\mathsf{wAND}_{1}:\mathsf{mem}
.

{subfigure}

Figure 9:
The
\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}
Pivot Policy.

Figure 10:
A boolean memory process.

Example B.40

(On Read/Write Process, reloaded)
.

Fig.
10
show that the processes
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}
conform to a policy
\mathsf{mem}
with
w_{i}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}r_{j}\in\mathsf{mem}
for
i\neq j
and
w_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}w_{0}\in\mathsf{mem}
. Obviously,
\mathsf{mem}
is a pivot policy. The processes
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}
are also coherent. One verifies by simple case analysis that for any derivative
Q
of
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}
, if

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}

and
\ell\not\in H
then there is a repetition

Q^{\prime}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime\prime}

with
H^{\prime}=H
. This yields Def.
12
. Secondly, suppose

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}Q_{1}

and

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}Q_{2}
.
Then, obviously, if
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
, the two transitions are never interference-free, because of the choice of blocking sets which orders all summation prefixes in a linear precedence order. If
Q_{1}\nequiv Q_{2}
then necessarily
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
. Thus, Def.
12
is satisfied, too, for trivial reasons.
So, the memory, which satisfies
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}\Vdash\mathsf{mem}
, is pivotable.

Note that
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}
is not a reflexive process, since none of its actions is self-blocking. We can put the memory in parallel with arbitrarily many writers and readers.
Consider a parallel composition
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}\,|\,P
where
P
is any single-threaded process that synchronises via
\overline{w_{i}}
for writing and
\overline{r_{i}}
for reading. Each writing action of
P
is a self-blocking prefix
\overline{w_{i}}{:}\overline{w_{i}}{\mathsf{.}}Q
and each reading is a choice
\overline{r_{1}}{:}\overline{r_{1}}{\mathsf{.}}R_{1}+\overline{r_{0}}{:}\{\overline{r_{0}},\overline{r_{1}}\}{\mathsf{.}}R_{0}
where the continuation processes
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
are selected according to the content of the memory, by matching with the
r_{0}
action of
\mathsf{wAND}_{0}
or the
r_{1}
action of
\mathsf{wAND}_{1}
. If the memory would offer both a synchronisation on
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
(which it does not), the reader would take the match on
r_{1}
, because of the precedences.
If
Q
,
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
follow the same principle, such a sequential program is a discrete process
P
that is coherent and conformant to the policy
\mathsf{prg}
with
\overline{r_{1}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{r_{i}}\in\mathsf{prg}
and
\overline{r_{0}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{r_{0}}\in\mathsf{prg}
.
Now we observe that the union
\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}
of policies is a pivot policy. Since
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}\Vdash\mathsf{mem}
and
P\Vdash\mathsf{prg}
we have
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}\Vdash\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}
as well as
P\Vdash\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}
. Then, by Prop.
D.82
we get

\mathsf{wAND}_{v}\,|\,P\Vdash\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}

i.e., the composition is coherent and pivotable.
In fact, we can put the memory in parallel with arbitrarily many sequential programs coherent for
\mathsf{prg}
and the composition
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}\,|\,P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}\,|\,\cdots\,|\,P_{n}
will be coherent for
\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}
. In particular, all reductions will be confluent under strong enabling. Technically, what happens is that first all writers of value
1
, synchronising as
w_{1}\,|\,\overline{w_{1}}
, will go ahead. Then all writers of
{{\mathsf{0}}}
, synchronising as
w_{0}\,|\,\overline{w_{0}}
become enabled. Only then the readers will be able to retrieve the final value via
r_{0}\,|\,\overline{r_{0}}
or
r_{1}\,|\,\overline{r_{1}}
rendezvous synchronisations. ∎

Observe that the pivot policy
\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}
of Ex.
B.40
permits a choice (and precedence) between both the receiver actions
w_{0}
,
w_{1}
but also between the sender actions
\overline{r_{0}}
,
\overline{r_{1}}
. As a result, the combined memory and program, which is coherent for
\mathsf{mem}\cup\mathsf{prg}
, is not an input-scheduled process in the sense of Def.
B.38
. This breaks the assumption on which the theory of classical prioritised process algebras like
[
10
]
or
[
36
]
are based. In reference to the former, Phillips writes:

They [Camilleri and Winskel] decide to sidestep this question by breaking the symmetry in CCS between inputs and outputs, and only allowing prioritised choice on input actions. We feel that this complicates the syntax and operational semantics,
and should not be necessary.
[
33
]

Our example and analysis indeed highlights the usefulness of the more general setting suggested by Phillips. In order to explain the theory of deterministic shared memory we need a larger class of processes than those considered in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
.

Note that all the examples of Sec.
3

have the “input-scheduled” property that
(non-trivial, i.e. non-self-blocking) precedences only exist between receiver actions
\mathcal{A}
, while sender actions
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
never appear in any choice contexts. These are the processes covered by
[
10
]
which restrict prioritised choice to receiver actions (“input-guarded, prioritised choice”) in the same way as the programming language
Occam
[
4
]
with its “PRIALT” construct does (cf.
[
10
]
).

Example B.41
.

The memory cell schedules readers after the writers and among the writers prefers to engage first with with those that set the value to
{{\mathsf{0}}}
, in the prioritised sum. This implements an Esterel-style resolution function for multi-writer convergecast, multi-reader broadcast of boolean signals. All concurrent readers receive the same value, which is the combined-AND of all values written by any concurrent writer. If any process writes a boolean
1
it will win the competition. This is also known as a “wired-AND”. Likewise, we can implement a memory cell that models a “wired-OR” if we swap the roles of
w_{0}
and
w_{1}
in the prioritised choices of
\mathsf{wAND}_{v}
.

Note that the wired-AND behaviour only applies to concurrent threads. A single thread can use the
\mathsf{wAND}
cell as a local memory and destructively update it sequentially as it wishes. Consider the difference: Both concurrent writers
