\overline{w_{1}}{:}\overline{w_{1}}\,|\,\overline{w_{0}}{:}\overline{w_{0}}\,|\,\mathsf{wAND}_{0}\,|\,R
and
\overline{w_{0}}{:}\overline{w_{0}}\,|\,\overline{w_{1}}{:}\overline{w_{1}}\,|\,\mathsf{wAND}_{0}\,|\,R
will necessarily make the reader
R\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}r_{0}{:}r_{1}{\mathsf{.}}A+r_{1}{:}\{r_{0},r_{1}\}{\mathsf{.}}B
detect a value
r_{0}
and therefore end in state
A
(wired-AND of
w_{0}
and
w_{1}
).
The two single-threaded sequential writings
\overline{w_{1}}{:}\overline{w_{1}}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w_{0}}{:}\overline{w_{0}}\,|\,\mathsf{wAND}_{0}\,|\,R
and
\overline{w_{0}}{:}\overline{w_{0}}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{w_{1}}{:}\overline{w_{1}}\,|\,\mathsf{wAND}_{0}\,|\,R
will generate two different readings by
R
: The first is the reading
r_{0}
, leading to
A
. The second is the reading
r_{1}
, leading to
B
. ∎

Strong Enabling vs Constructive Enabling

As an intermediate step to implement our precedence-based scheduling, the previous example suggests we look at the
weak initial actions
for a process.

The next example shows why strong enabling, based on
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(R)
, is not sufficient and why we need the stronger notion of c-enabling (Def.
3
), based on
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R)
.

Example B.42

(On Multi-Cast and Constructive Enabling)
.

Suppose
S_{0}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}a{:}e{\mathsf{.}}S_{0}+e{\mathsf{.}}S_{1}+\sigma{:}\{a,e\}{\mathsf{.}}S_{0}
is an Esterel Signal (Sec.
9
) in its absent state, with abbreviations
e=\mathit{emit}
,
a=\mathit{abs}
and
p=\mathit{pres}
. Its initial action

S_{0}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{a}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e\}}}\,$}S_{0}

to communicate absence does not block itself to permit multi-reader scenarios. A typical reader is of form
R\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{p}{:}\overline{p}{\mathsf{.}}R_{1}+\overline{a}{:}\{\overline{a},\overline{p}\}{\mathsf{.}}R_{0}
. This is a choice: if the signal is present we execute
R_{1}
, otherwise if it is absent we execute
R_{0}
. The two sender actions
\overline{p}
and
\overline{a}
are self-blocking, because these prefixes can only be consumed once. In essence, what we want is that there can be many senders of the value labels
\overline{p}
,
\overline{a}
(readers) but at most one receiver for
p
,
a
(signal).

Consider a reader
C\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{a}{:}\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}
that wants to emit the signal when it finds it absent. The composition
S_{0}\,|\,C
is deterministic under strong enabling.

S_{0}\,|\,C\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,\overline{a}\}}}\,$}S_{0}\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}.

The blocking set contains two labels:

{bracketenumerate}

First,
e\in\{e,\overline{a}\}
indicates priority, i.e., that we do not want another concurrent thread sending
\overline{e}
, because the synchronisation for absence is based on the assumption that there is no emission.
Specifically,

S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,\overline{e}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{e}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,\overline{a}\}}}\,$}S_{0}\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}\,|\,\overline{e}

will block because
\{e,\overline{a}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}({{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{e})\neq\{\,\}
.
Instead, the transition

S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,\overline{e}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,C\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{e}\}}}\,$}S_{1}\,|\,C\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}

which emits the signal can proceed.

Second,
\overline{a}\in\{e,\overline{a}\}
indicates that the sending prefix
\overline{a}{:}\overline{a}
of the reader
C
(which has entered into the synchronisation
\tau=a\,|\,\overline{a}
) must not be consumed by another competing receiver.
Specifically,

S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,a\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,a}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,\overline{a}\}}}\,$}S_{0}\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}\,|\,a

will block.

Now, take
S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,D
where the signal
S_{0}
is connected with two readers, where
C
is as above and
D\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{a}{:}\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}D_{1}
analogous. Both readers can go ahead and synchronise in reactions (
\tau=a\,|\,\overline{a}
)

S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,D\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,\overline{a}\}}}\,$}S_{0}\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}\,|\,D

and

S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,D\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,C\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,\overline{a}\}}}\,$}S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}D_{1}

which are both strongly enabled since

\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}({{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D)=\{a\}=\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}({{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,C\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}})
which is disjoint from the blocking set
\{e,\overline{a}\}
.
This may seem ok, as we wanted to have multiple readers access the signal on the same port
a
. However, now the emission of
\overline{e}
by the process that advanced first will block the reading of absence by the other which stayed behind. In each case symmetrically, there is only one (strongly enabled) transition left, viz.

S_{0}\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}\,|\,D\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{e}\}}}\,$}S_{1}\,|\,C_{1}\,|\,D

and

S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}D_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,C\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{e}\}}}\,$}S_{1}\,|\,C\,|\,D_{1}

where the final states
C_{1}\,|\,D
and
C\,|\,D_{1}
of the readers are behaviourally different. Thus, we have non-determinism while the confluence is lost.

The core of the problem is that the concurrent contexts
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D
and
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,C\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}
, respectively, of the above rendezvous synchronisations and do not account for the fact that the signal
S_{0}
can be simultaneously reused by the other reader that is not involved in the transition.
We could account for this reuse by adding
S_{0}
into the concurrent context as in

S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,D\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{S_{0}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,\overline{a}\}}}\,$}

S_{0}\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}\,|\,D.
Now
D
can interact with
S_{0}
in the concurrent context
S_{0}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D
. The above transition
depends on the assumption that no other thread will send
\overline{e}
. This is not true, because now
e\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(S_{0}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D)
. Thus, putting the shared signal
S_{0}
into the concurrent environment is enforcing determinism by blocking the strong enabling
properties of the above transitions.
Unfortunately, the fix is too strong. The presence of
S_{0}
in the concurrent context would block even the single reader in
S_{0}\,|\,\overline{a}{:}\overline{a}
.
Specifically, the reduction

S_{0}\,|\,\overline{a}{:}\overline{a}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{S_{0}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,a\}}}\,$}

S_{0}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}

would block, because
\overline{a}\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}(S_{0})\subseteq\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(S_{0}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}})
, and so the reduction
is not strongly enabled any more. Thus, our fix would not permit any reading of the signal
S_{0}
, whatsoever.

The solution is to tighten up the notion of strong enabling and close it under arbitrary non-clock transitions in the concurrent environment rather than just the silent steps. Technically, we define a larger set
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)\supseteq\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\tau}}(E)
of potential labels (Def.
3
) that closes under all non-clock actions of
E
.
In this case, we do find
e\in\{e,\overline{a}\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}({{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,D)\neq\{\,\}
.
In this way, diverging reductions
are no longer enabled. On the other hand, the reduction

S_{0}\,|\,C\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{e,a\}}}\,$}S_{0}\,|\,\overline{e}{:}\overline{e}{\mathsf{.}}C_{1}

remains enabled, because
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}({{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}})=\{\,\}
.
In general, single readers
S_{0}\,|\,C
where
C
is an arbitrary sequential process, or multiple readers
S_{0}\,|\,C\,|\,D
where at most one of
C
or
D
is writing, i.e., sending
\overline{e}
, remain enabled. ∎

Example B.43

(On Fully Multi-Cast
\mathsf{ABRO}
)
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{ABRO}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sigma{\mathsf{.}}(\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B}\,|\,\mathsf{R}\,|\,\mathsf{O})\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle k{:}k{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+a{:}\{k,a\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{s}{:}\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+\sigma{:}\{k,a\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{A}

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle k{:}k{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+b{:}\{k,b\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{s}{:}\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+\sigma{:}\{k,b\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{B}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle r{:}r{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{R}^{\prime}+\tau{:}{r}{\mathsf{.}}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{R}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\hskip-1.13809pt

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\overline{k}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{R}^{\prime}+\tau{:}\overline{k}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{ABRO}

\displaystyle\mathsf{O}\hskip 0.7113pt

\displaystyle\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle k{:}k{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+s{:}k{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{O}+t{:}\{k,t,s\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{o}{:}\overline{o}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}+\sigma{:}\{k,s,t\}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{O}.

Figure 11
:
Simplified version of
\mathsf{ABRO}
.

To model full multi-cast
\mathsf{ABRO}
in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, we could use sharable (i.e., non-self-blocking) prefixes on internal actions to show how they are useful to model the control-flow of Esterel programs, specifically termination and reset.
Consider the process
\mathsf{ABRO}
from Ex.
10
again:

•

The reset watchdog
\mathsf{R}
can receive a reset signal
r
or silently move to state
\sigma{\mathsf{.}}R
where it synchronises on the clock
\sigma
to repeat in the next macro-step. When a reset
r
is received by the environment, then
\mathsf{R}
executes the sequence

r{:}r{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{1}{:}\overline{k}_{1}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{2}{:}\overline{k}_{2}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{3}{:}\overline{k}_{3}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{k}_{4}{:}\overline{k}_{4}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{ABRO}
whereby it sends a “kill” signal
\overline{k}_{i}
to each of the four threads
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{O}
, in some arbitrary but fixed order. When all kills have been delivered, the watchdog
\mathsf{R}
will restart
\mathsf{ABRO}
. Until such time, there is no clock possible. The second transition

R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{r\}}}\,$}\sigma{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{R}

preempts this reset choice
but has lower priority as it is blocked by
r
. It is only taken when there is no reset possible, i.e., when the reset signal
r
is absent. Note that with the preemption of the
the kill signals
\overline{k}_{i}
are removed from the potential, because
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(\sigma{\mathsf{.}}R)=\{\,\}
, i.e., we do not look past the clock prefix.

•

The
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
processes are analogous to each other with the role of
a
and
b
swapped. Looking at
\mathsf{A}
, we see that it has a choice of three actions, viz to receive a kill signal
k_{1}
, an input signal
a
or perform the clock action
\sigma
to start the next macro step. The kill
k_{1}
has highest priority, action
a
second and the clock can only go ahead if there is neither a
k_{1}
nor an
a
signal to be received. When there is no kill but an
a
signal, then the process
\mathsf{A}
sends an
\overline{s}
signal to indicate its termination. When the kill
k_{1}
occurs, it enters into the halt state
{{\mathsf{0}}}
where it is inactive but permits any number of clock ticks. Since this is the neutral element for parallel composition,
\mathsf{A}
essentially drops out of the picture. Note that we cannot use the inactive
{{\mathsf{0}}}
here, because then the terminated process
\mathsf{A}
would block the clock for all other threads. Finally, when there is neither a kill
k_{1}
nor signal
a
, the
\mathsf{A}
process can synchronise on
\sigma
and repeat as
\mathsf{A}
in the next macro-step. In this fashion,
\mathsf{A}
will patiently wait for a clock tick in which
k_{1}
or
a
occur. If both occur, the kill takes priority: For instance, the transition

\mathsf{A}\,|\,\overline{k}_{1}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{1}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{k_{1}\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}

is constructively enabled while

\mathsf{A}\,|\,\overline{k}_{1}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{k}_{1}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{k_{1},a\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,\overline{k}_{1}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,{{\mathsf{0}}}

is not enabled.

•

The process
\mathsf{T}
is the termination detector implementing the principle discussed above. It forms a clock-patient, killable counter that waits for any possible reception of signal
s
before it sends termination
\overline{t}
to process
\mathsf{O}
. Note that
\mathsf{T}
itself does not need to know how many possible termination signals there are. It waits for the termination count (implemented by
\textsf{iA}^{\ast}
) to reach zero.

•

When process
\mathsf{O}
receives the termination signal
t
it sends the output signal
\overline{o}
to the environment and then halts. Alternatively, with highest priority, it can be killed by reception of
k_{5}
or engage in the clock
\sigma
if neither a kill
k_{5}
nor a termination
t
is present.
The reduction
\tau=\overline{t}{:}\{k_{3},\overline{t},s\}\,|\,t{:}\{k_{4},t\}
between
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{O}
will be blocked by precedence, for as long as at least one of the processes
\mathsf{A}
or
\mathsf{B}
has a choice term that sends
\overline{s}
, i.e., as long as
s\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B})
. Only when both prefix sequences
a{:}\{a,k_{1}\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{s}{:}\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
b{:}\{b,k_{2}\}{\mathsf{.}}\overline{s}{:}\overline{s}{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
have been eliminated by reductions, then the termination can occur.

Using multi-cast, we can improve on this as seen in Fig.
11
:
We can integrate the termination test into the output process
\mathsf{O}
if we let
\mathsf{O}
loop on consumption of
s
rather than
\mathsf{T}
. Similarly, we can use only a single (multi-cast) kill signal
k
shared between
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{O}
: The reset process
\mathsf{R}
keeps sending
\overline{k}
unboundedly often, until all target processes have received a copy and all receptions
k
have been consumed. At that point, it takes a clock step to repeat
\mathsf{ABRO}
from the start.

One can verify that
\mathsf{ABRO}
is coherent and pivotable using Thm.
24
.

•

The choices in each of the threads
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{R}
,
\mathsf{O}
are fully resolved by the blocking sets, i.e., every pair of actions in the summations are distinct and in precedence relationships. Every rendezvous prefix is self-blocking unless the action can be repeated with the same blocking in the continuation process. This happens for channels
s
and
k
in the recursions
\mathsf{O}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\ldots+s{:}k{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{O}+\ldots
and
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\stackrel{{{}_{\mathit{def}}}}{{=}}\overline{k}{\mathsf{.}}\mathsf{R}^{\prime}+\ldots
. Thus, the threads
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{R}
,
\mathsf{O}
themselves are coherent.

•

A suitable policy
\pi
, for which the threads
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{R}
,
\mathsf{O}
are coherent, enforces the precedences

–

k\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\pi
for
\ell\in\{k,a,b,s,t,\sigma\}

–

\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\pi
for
\ell\in\{r,k,a,b,\overline{s},t,\overline{o}\}

–

s\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}t\in\pi

–

\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\sigma\in\pi
for
\ell\in\{a,b,s,t\}
.

The dual matching pairs for these precedences, however, are all concurrently independent. More precisely, we look at distinct labels,
\overline{k}\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{\ell}\in\pi
for all
\ell\in\{r,o,a,b,s,t,\sigma\}
,
\overline{s}\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{t}\in\pi
and
\overline{\ell}\mathrel{\diamond}\sigma\in\pi
for
\ell\in\{r,o,a,b,s,t\}
.

•

Thus, the policy
\pi
(and thus
\pi\,\backslash\,\mathcal{R}
) is a pivot policy and so the parallel composition
\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B}\,|\,\mathsf{R}\,|\,\mathsf{O}
is coherent for
\pi
. Further,
\pi
is precedence-closed for
\{s,t\}
which means that
(\mathsf{A}\,|\,\mathsf{B}\,|\,\mathsf{R}\,|\,\mathsf{O})\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}
is coherent for

\pi\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}
. Finally, since
\{\}\subseteq\{\ell\mid\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\sigma\in\pi\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}\}=\{a,b\}
, the process
\mathsf{ABRO}
(which starts with a prefix
\sigma{:}H
where
H=\{\,\}
) is coherent for
\pi\,\backslash\,\{s,t\}
. ∎

Appendix C
Auxiliary Results

In the following we list some simple observation on the properties of
\textsf{iA}(P)
,
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)
and
\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)
that will be used in the proofs.

Lemma C.44
.

Let
P
,
Q
be processes:

{bracketenumerate}

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q
implies
R\equiv{{\mathsf{0}}}
.

\alpha\in\textsf{iA}(P)
iff
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q
.

\textsf{iA}(P)\cap\mathcal{R}\subseteq\textsf{iA}(P\,|\,Q)
.

If
P
is single-threaded, then
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q
implies
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}
,
R\equiv{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
\tau\not\in H
.

If
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q
then
\textsf{iA}(R)\subseteq\textsf{iA}(P)
.

Proof C.45
.

(2) is by definition. The other points follow by an inspection of the SOS rules, using the definition of
\textsf{iA}(P)
.

Lem.
C.44
(1) means that clock actions always run in an empty concurrent environment. Observe that Lem.
C.44
(2) means that the set
\textsf{iA}(P)
corresponds to the initial actions of
P
.
The presence of
\tau
in the blocking set of a transition
provides information about the set of initial actions of a process. Specifically, in rule
(\textit{Com})
the presence of
\tau
in the set
H=race(P\,|\,Q)

indicates a blocking situation arising from the priorities in
P
or
Q
conflicting with the rendevous actions
\ell
or
\overline{\ell}
. Thus, the condition
\tau\not\in H
can be used to implement priority-based scheduling.
Lem.
C.44
(4) implies that single-threaded processes are never blocked.

Lemma C.46
.

Let
P
be an arbitrary process:

{bracketenumerate}

\textsf{iA}(P)\cap\mathcal{L}\subseteq\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)\subseteq\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)
.

If
P\equiv Q
then
\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)=\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(Q)
.

If
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{}\,$}Q
for
\alpha\in\mathcal{R}\cup\{\tau\}
, then
\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(Q)\subseteq\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)
.

Proof C.47
.

All points follow by an inspection of the SOS rules, using the definition of
\textsf{iA}^{\tau}(P)
and
\textsf{iA}^{\ast}(P)
.

On Interference

Proposition C.48
.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}{:}H_{1}[E_{1}\,|\,R]
and
\alpha_{2}{:}H_{2}[E_{2}\,|\,R]
are interference-free. Then,
\alpha_{1}{:}H_{1}^{\prime}[E_{1}]
and
\alpha_{2}{:}H_{2}^{\prime}[E_{2}]
are interference-free for arbitrary subsets
H_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{1}
and
H_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{2}
.

As a special case, choosing
R={{\mathsf{0}}}
, Proposition
C.48
implies non-interference of
\alpha_{i}{:}H_{i}^{\prime}[E_{i}]
for
i\in\{1,2\}
from non-interference of
\alpha_{i}{:}H_{i}[E_{i}]
for any
H_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{i}
.

Proof C.49
.

First, non-interference of
\alpha_{i}{:}H_{i}[E_{i}\,|\,R]
means
\alpha_{i}\not\in H_{3-i}
by assumption. Since
H_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{i}
, this implies also
\alpha_{i}\not\in H_{3-i}^{\prime}
. Second, suppose that
\alpha_{3-i}=\tau
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then, non-interference of
\alpha_{1}{:}H_{1}[E_{1}\,|\,R]
and
\alpha_{2}\overline{H}_{2}[E_{2}\,|\,R]
gives
\tau\not\in H_{i}
and
H_{i}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E_{i}\,|\,R)=\{\,\}
.
Since by Lem.
C.44
,
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E_{i})\subseteq\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E_{i}\,|\,R)
, the inclusions
H_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{i}
preserve this property, i.e.,
\tau\not\in H_{i}^{\prime}
and
H_{i}^{\prime}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E_{i})\subseteq H_{i}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E_{i}\,|\,R)=\{\,\}
.

On Policies

We obtain a partial ordering
\pi_{1}\preceq\pi_{2}
on precedence policies by subset inclusion. Specifically, we have
(L_{1},\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{1}$})\preceq(L_{2},\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{2}$})
if
L_{1}\subseteq L_{2}

such that for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L_{1}
,
if
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{2}$}\ell_{2}
then
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{1}$}\ell_{2}
.
Intuitively, if
\pi_{1}\preceq\pi_{2}
then
\pi_{2}
is a tightening of
\pi_{1}
in the sense that it exports more labels (resources) subject to possibly fewer precedences (causality constraints) than
\pi_{1}
.
By way of this ordering, we can use policies to measure the degree of concurrency exhibited by a process.
The alphabet of the
\preceq
-maximal policy
\pi_{max}
contains all labels
\mathcal{L}
and its precedence relation
\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{max}
is empty.
It is the largest policy in the
\preceq
ordering.
The
\preceq
-minimal policy
\pi_{min}
has empty alphabet and empty precedences. It is self-dual, i.e.,
\overline{\pi}_{min}=\pi_{min}
.

There is also the normal inclusion ordering
\pi_{1}\subseteq\pi_{2}
between policies defined by
L_{1}\subseteq L_{2}
and
\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{1}$}\subseteq\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;\!\!_{2}$}
. It differs from
\preceq
in the inclusion direction of the precedence alphabets.

For any policy
\pi
on alphabet
L
we define its
dual
as a policy
\overline{\pi}
on the set of labels
\overline{L}

where for all
\overline{\ell}_{1},\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\overline{L}
we stipulate
\overline{\ell}_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\overline{\pi}
iff
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
or
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}\in\pi
, i.e., both
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\not\in\pi
and
\ell_{2}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{1}\not\in\pi
. Intuitively, the dual policy
\overline{\pi}
consists of matching copies
\overline{\ell}
of all labels
\ell
from
\pi
and puts them in a precedence relation precisely if their original copies are identical or do not stand in precedence to each other, in any direction. This generates a form of “negation”
\overline{\pi}
from
\pi
, which is reflexive and symmetric.
Note that
\overline{\overline{\pi}}
need not be identical to
\pi

but contains more precedences. In general, we have
\overline{\overline{\pi}}\preceq\pi
and
\overline{\overline{\pi}}=\pi
if
\pi
is symmetric. The policy
\overline{\pi}
captures the independences between actions of
\pi
and is called the associated
independence alphabet
, in the sense of trace theory
[
12
]
. Analogously,
\overline{\overline{\pi}}
expresses the dependencies of actions in
\pi
, called the associated
dependence alphabet
.

Example C.50
.

Suppose
P\Vdash\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}
then all transitions with labels
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
of
P
must be from concurrent threads and thus cannot block each other. For instance, both
\ell_{1}\,|\,\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
conform to
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}
. However, the choice
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
is not coherent. It becomes coherent if we add a precedence
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}{:}\ell_{1}
but then it is not longer conformant to
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}
. Instead, we now have
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}{:}\ell_{1}\Vdash\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}
, where the policy expresses the precedence coded in the blocking sets of the prefixes.
Suppose
P\Vdash\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}
then all transitions with labels
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
of
P
must be from concurrent threads and thus cannot block each other. For instance, both
\ell_{1}\,|\,\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
conform to
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}
. However, the choice
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
is not coherent. It becomes coherent if we add a precedence
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}{:}\ell_{1}
but then it is no longer conformant to
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}
. Instead, we now have
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}{:}\ell_{1}\Vdash\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}
, where the policy expresses the precedence coded in the blocking sets of the prefixes.
More generally, if
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi
and
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
are distinct, then
P\Vdash\pi
may take
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
transitions to completely different states from the same thread.

Definition C.51

(Pivot Policy)
.

A policy
\pi
is called a pivot policy if
\overline{\pi}\preceq\pi
.
We call a process
P
pivotable if
P
conforms to a pivot policy.

Proposition C.52
.

A policy
\pi=(L,\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$})
is a pivot policy if

\overline{L}\subseteq L
and for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L
with
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
we have
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}\in\pi
or
\overline{\ell}_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\pi
.

Proof C.53
.

Let
\pi
be a pivot policy. Since the alphabet of
\overline{\pi}
is
\overline{L}
and the alphabet of
\pi
is
L
the assumption
\overline{\pi}\preceq\pi
implies
\overline{L}\subseteq L
by definition of
\preceq
. Given labels
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L
with
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
suppose
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}\not\in\pi
. Hence
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi
or
\ell_{2}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi
. Consider the first case, i.e.,
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi
. Since
\overline{L}\subseteq L
and
L=\overline{\overline{L}}
it follows that also
L\subseteq\overline{L}
, i.e., the labels
\ell_{i}
are also in the alphabet of
\overline{\pi}
. But then by definition of the ordering and the fact that
\ell_{i}=\overline{\overline{\ell}}_{i}
, the assumption
\overline{\pi}\preceq\pi
implies that
\overline{\overline{\ell}}_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\overline{\ell}}_{2}\in\overline{\pi}
. Now, the definition of the dual policy means that
\overline{\ell}_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\ell}_{2}\not\in\pi
and
\overline{\ell}_{2}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\ell}_{1}\not\in\pi
. This is the same as
\overline{\ell}_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\pi
as desired.

Suppose the properties (1) and (2) of the proposition hold for a policy
\pi
. We claim that
\overline{\pi}\preceq\pi
. The inclusion of alphabets is by assumption (1). Then, suppose
\overline{\ell}_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\pi
which implies

\overline{\ell}_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\overline{\ell}_{2}\not\in\pi
. If
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
we have
\overline{\ell}_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\overline{\pi}
directly by definition. So, let
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
.
Because of
\overline{L}\subseteq L
and (2) this gives us
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}\in\pi
considering again that
\ell_{i}=\overline{\overline{\ell}}_{i}
.
But
\ell_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell_{2}\in\pi
is the same as
\overline{\ell}_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\ell}_{2}\in\overline{\pi}
by definition.

Proposition C.54
.

Suppose
P
is input-scheduled
\pi_{is}
and
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q
for some
\alpha
,
H
,
R
,
Q
. Then:

1.

If
\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
then
H\subseteq\{\alpha,\tau\}
.

2.

If
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\{\tau\}
then
H\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup\{\tau\}
.

Proof C.55
.

Obvious by definition of conformance and the construction of
\pi_{is}
.

The next crucial Lemma
implies that two coherent threads cannot block each other if they are conformant to the same pivot policy. The blocking of a reduction
\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}
must always arise from a thread in the concurrent context that is not involved in the synchronisation.

Lemma C.56
.

Let
P_{1}\Vdash\pi
and
P_{2}\Vdash\pi
be coherent for the same pivot policy
\pi
and
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
such that

P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\ell}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}P_{2}^{\prime}.
Then,
H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R_{1})=\{\,\}
implies
H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{1})\subseteq\{\overline{\ell}\}
.

Proof C.57
.

Let
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
and the transitions be given as in the statement of the Lemma. Suppose
\alpha\in H_{2}
and
\alpha\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{1})
. We claim that
\alpha=\overline{\ell}
, whence
H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{1})\subseteq\{\overline{\ell}\}
.
By contraposition let us assume that
\alpha\neq\overline{\ell}
, or equivalently
\overline{\alpha}\neq\ell
. Further,
since
\textsf{iA}(P_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{L}
we also have
\alpha\neq\tau
. The assumption
\alpha\in H_{2}
means
\alpha\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\ell}\in\pi
by Def.
12
(1) applied to
P_{2}
. Since
\pi
is pivot we must thus have
\overline{\alpha}\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell\in\pi
, specifically
\overline{\alpha}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\not\in\pi
, i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}\not\in H_{1}
, again by Def.
12
(1), now applied to
P_{1}
.
But since
\overline{\alpha}\in\textsf{iA}(P_{1})
, by Lem.
C.44
, there is a transition

P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\alpha}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q.
Since also
\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{\alpha}\not\in\pi
, by pivot property, it follows
\ell\not\in H
by Def.
12
(1) again for
P_{1}
. Now both
\overline{\alpha}\not\in H_{1}
and
\ell\not\in H
mean that the c-actions
\ell{:}H_{1}[R_{1}]
and
\overline{\alpha}{:}H[E]
are interference-free (Def.
D.60
). Since
\ell\neq\overline{\alpha}
there must exist a reconvergent process
R
and transitions

P_{1}^{\prime}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\alpha}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}R\text{ and }Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}^{\prime}}}\,$}R\text{ with }R_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}R_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }E\xrightarrow{\ell}E^{\prime}.

The latter transitions are strengthenings of the residuals
R_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\alpha}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}R_{1}^{\prime}
and
E\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}E^{\prime}
that must exist according to the statement of Coherence, because of
\{\ell,\overline{\alpha}\}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
and
\ell\neq\overline{\alpha}
. However, the transition
R_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}R_{1}^{\prime}
implies
\overline{\alpha}\in\textsf{iA}(R_{1})
which is impossible, because then
\alpha\in H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R_{1})
while
H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R_{1})=\{\,\}
by assumption.

We can show that pivotable processes do not have internal races.

Lemma C.58
.

Let
P\Vdash\pi
be coherent for a pivot policy
\pi
. Then, for every reduction

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime},
if
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(E)=\{\,\}
then
\tau\not\in H
.

Proof C.59
.

Let
P
be coherent for pivotable
\pi
and a reduction

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}

such that
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(E)=\{\,\}
be given. Suppose, by contraposition,
\tau\in H
. Since there are no action prefixes
\tau{\mathsf{.}}P^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, the reduction must arise from a synchronisation of two threads
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
inside
P
, via rule
(\textit{Com})
, i.e.,

P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}\xrightarrow[R_{1}\,|\,R_{2}]{\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}\cup B}}\,P_{1}^{\prime}\,|\,P_{2}^{\prime}\lx@proof@logical@and P_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P_{1}^{\prime}P_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\ell}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}P_{2}^{\prime}B=\{\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}\mid H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{1})\not\subseteq\{\overline{\ell}\}\text{ or }H_{1}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{2})\not\subseteq\{\ell\}\}

for some
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
. The assumption
H\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(E)=\{\,\}
for the top-level reduction implies that
(H_{1}\cup H_{2})\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R_{1}\,|\,R_{2})=\{\,\}
at the point of
(\textit{Com})
. Here we may assume, by induction on the depth of the derivation, that
\tau\not\in H_{1}\cup H_{2}
.
We must show that the synchronisation action
\tau
cannot enter into the blocking set by
(\textit{Com})
, i.e.,
\tau=\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}\not\in B
, i.e., which is the same as
H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{1})\subseteq\{\overline{\ell}\}
and
H_{1}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{2})\subseteq\{\ell\}
.
The sub-expressions
P_{1}\Vdash\pi^{\prime}
and
P_{2}\Vdash\pi^{\prime}
are also coherent for a common pivot policy
\pi^{\prime}
. This policy may contain local labels that are restricted or hidden in the outer process
P
. Since by assumption we have

H_{1}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R_{1})\subseteq(H_{1}\cup H_{2})\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R_{1}\,|\,R_{2})=\{\,\}
and likewise
H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(R_{2})=\{\,\}
, we can apply Lem.
C.56
to show
H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{1})\subseteq\{\overline{\ell}\}
and
H_{1}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{2})\subseteq\{\ell\}
. This means
B=\{\,\}
and we are done.

The Role of Pivotability

Let us give an example to show that the requirement of pivotability cannot be removed in the closure statement for parallel compositions of Thm.
14
.
For compactness of notation let us write
\underline{\ell}{:}H
for
\ell{:}(H\cup\{\ell\})
to create reflexive prefixes.
Let

Q=\underline{b}\,|\,\underline{c}{:}a\text{ and }R=\underline{\overline{b}}{:}\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}R_{1}+\underline{\overline{c}}{:}\overline{a}{\mathsf{.}}R_{2}\text{ with }R_{1}=\underline{\overline{a}}+\underline{\overline{c}}{:}\overline{a}\text{ and }R_{2}=\underline{\overline{a}}+\overline{\underline{b}}{:}\overline{a}.

First, we observe that both
Q
and
R
are coherent:

•

Process
Q
has two admissible transitions

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{b}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{b\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}\text{ and }Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{c}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{c,a\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}

which both are self-interfering. Thus, reconvergence is required only between the two transitions which is guaranteed, because they are from concurrent threads. This can also be obtained from Thm.
24
and the pivot policy with
b\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}b\in\pi
,
c\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}c\in\pi
and
a\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}c\in\pi
.

•

Process
R
has two admissible transitions, the c-action
R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\underline{\overline{b}}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{a}\}}}\,$}R_{1}
and
R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\underline{\overline{c}}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{a}\}}}\,$}R_{2}
. Each interferes with itself but not with the other. Thus, coherence implies that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
reconverge. This is indeed the case, since we have
R_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\underline{\overline{c}}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{a}\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
R_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\underline{\overline{b}}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{\overline{a}\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}
, with commuting actions. The concurrent contexts are residual since
{{\mathsf{0}}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{c}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
{{\mathsf{0}}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{b}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
. Since all admissible transitions of
R_{1}
(and
R_{2}
) are reflexive and interfere with each other,
R_{1}
(and
R_{2}
) are coherent, too.

Next we show that the parallel composition
Q\,|\,R
is not coherent. We have admissible c-actions

Q\,|\,R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\underline{c}{:}a}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{b\,|\,\overline{b}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{b,\overline{b},\overline{a}\}}}\,$}\underline{c}{:}a\,|\,R_{1}\text{ and }Q\,|\,R\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\underline{b}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{c\,|\,\overline{c}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{c,\overline{c},a,\overline{a}\}}}\,$}\underline{b}\,|\,R_{2}.

which are in fact c-enabled. However, the continuation process
\underline{c}{:}a\,|\,R_{1}=\underline{c}{:}a\,|\,\underline{\overline{a}}+\underline{\overline{c}}{:}\overline{a}
on the left is binary blocked by a race condition. We find

\underline{c}{:}a\,|\,R_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{c\,|\,\overline{c}}$}}\!\!{{}_{\{c,\overline{c},\overline{a},\tau\}}}\,$}{{\mathsf{0}}}

where the silent
\tau
enters the blocking set, because
\{a\}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(\underline{\overline{a}}+\underline{\overline{c}}{:}\overline{a})\not\subseteq\{c\}
. This is a race computed in rule
(\textit{Com})
. Thus, the reduction
c\,|\,\overline{c}
of the original state
Q\,|\,R
is not longer c-enabled in the state
\underline{c}{:}a\,|\,R_{1}
. This breaks coherence.

Finally, we notice that
Q\,|\,R
is not pivotable.
A common policy
\pi
for
Q\,|\,R
must obviously include the precedences
\overline{a}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\overline{c}\in\pi
, as well as
a\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}c\in\pi
. This is not pivot according to Def.
C.51
.

On Milner’ Confluence Class

As we have noted above,
CCS
corresponds to the unclocked and (blocking) free processes of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
(Prop.
B.29
). For these processes, admissible transitions and strongly enabled transitions coincide.
The key result of Milner
[
27
]
(Chap. 11, Prop. 19) is that confluence is preserved by inaction
{{\mathsf{0}}}
, prefixing
\ell{\mathsf{.}}P
and

confluent composition
defined as

P\mathrel{|_{L}}Q=(P\,|\,Q)\,\backslash\,L

where
\mathcal{L}(P)\cap\mathcal{L}(Q)=\{\,\}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}(P)}\cap\mathcal{L}(Q)\subseteq L\cup\overline{L}
.
We will show how confluence for this fragment of
CCS
processes follows from our more general results on coherence. From now on, for the rest of this section, we assume that all processes are unclocked, i.e., each prefix
\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
has
H\cup\{\ell\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\overline{\mathcal{A}}
.

Let us call a process
P

discrete
if all prefixes occurring in
P
are of form
\ell{:}\{\ell\}{\mathsf{.}}Q
, i.e., they are self-blocking. Under strong enabling, a discrete process behaves like a
CCS
process with the restriction that each action
\ell
only synchronises if there is a matching partner
\overline{\ell}
in a
unique
other parallel thread. If the matching partner is not unique, then the scheduling blocks. For instance,
\ell{:}\ell\,|\,\overline{\ell}{:}\overline{\ell}\,|\,\overline{\ell}{:}\overline{\ell}
blocks because there are two concurrent senders
\overline{\ell}
matching the receiver
\ell
.
Now consider the fragment
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
of discrete processes built from inaction
{{\mathsf{0}}}
, self-blocking prefixes
\ell{:}\{\ell\}{\mathsf{.}}Q
and confluent composition.
Milner’s result says that the processes in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
are confluent under admissible scheduling.

To emulate Milner’s result in our setting, we consider the
sort

\mathcal{L}(P)
of a process
P
as the admissible actions of a
discrete
policy
\pi_{P}
with only reflexive precedences. In other words,
\ell_{1}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell_{2}\in\pi_{P}
iff
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
.
It is easy to see that a discrete process always conforms to
\pi_{P}
.
Also, discrete policies are always pivot policies (Def.
C.51
) and dependency-closed for all label sets (Def.
C.51
), as one shows easily. Thus, discrete processes, which are coherent for discrete policies, fullfill all conditions of the preservation laws for inaction
{{\mathsf{0}}}
(Prop.
D.74
), self-blocking action prefix
\ell{:}\{\ell\}{\mathsf{.}}Q
(Prop.
D.76
), parallel composition
P\,|\,Q
(Prop.
D.82
) and restriction
P\,\backslash\,L
(Prop.
D.86
).
As a consequence and in particular, all processes in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
are coherent under strong enabling by our results.

Our final observation now is that, for Milner’s fragment
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
, the transition semantics under admissible and strong enabling, as well as the notions of confluence and coherence, coincide.
Firstly, if
P
is discrete then in every c-action
\ell{:}H[E]
executed by a derivative
Q
of
P
we must have
H=\{\ell\}
. Thus, the coherence Def.
12
(1) becomes redundant since it is always satisfied.
Further, any two c-actions
\ell_{i}{:}H_{i}[E_{i}]
of transitions

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}Q_{1}\text{ and }Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-1.99306pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-3.98611pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}Q_{2}
for
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
or
Q_{1}\not\equiv Q_{2}
are trivially interference-free. The confluent composition
of Milner has the further effect that for every c-action
\ell{:}H[E]
we must have
\ell\not\in\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E)
. This is proven by induction on the structure of
P\in\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
. Further, the side condition of
(\textit{Com})
becomes trivial, and thus generally
\tau\not\in H
. This is a result of the side-conditions of
the confluent composition.
Hence, for
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{cc}}
, strong enabling coincides with plain admissibility
CCS
.
Notice that the class of discrete processes and the class processes coherent for discrete policies is larger than
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{CW}}
. In particular, we can explain coherence of broadcast actions with repetitive prefixes. Hence, our results are more general even for the restricted class of discrete behaviours.

Appendix D
Proofs

First, we adopt a slightly more general form of interference, for general c-actions not just for those that are constructively enabled, as in Def.
11
.

Definition D.60
.

Two c-actions
\alpha_{1}{:}H_{1}[E_{1}]
and
\alpha_{2}{:}H_{2}[E_{2}]
are called interference-free if the following holds for all
i\in\{1,2\}
:

{bracketenumerate}

If
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
then
\alpha_{i}\not\in H_{3-i}
.

If
\alpha_{3-i}=\tau
then
H_{i}\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(E_{i})\cup\{\tau\})=\{\,\}
.

The extra condition (2) in Def.
D.60
says that for a c-action not to interfere with a silent (and thus uncontrollable) action it must be c-enabled. Obviously, Def.
D.60
is a weakening of Def.
11
, i.e. if
\alpha_{1}{:}H_{1}[E_{1}]
and
\alpha_{2}{:}H_{2}[E_{2}]
are interference-free according to Def.
D.60
they are interference-free according to Def.
11
.

Proposition D.61
.

If a coherent process
P
offers enabled transitions on a clock
\sigma
and another distinct action
\alpha\neq\sigma
with

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}P_{1}\text{ and }P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}P_{2}
then

\alpha\in H_{2}
or
\sigma\in H_{1}
.
In particular, if
\alpha=\tau
then
\sigma\in H_{1}
.

Proof D.62
.

Since
R_{2}={{\mathsf{0}}}
by Lem.
C.44
(1) and
\textsf{iA}({{\mathsf{0}}})=\{\,\}
, we have
\alpha\not\in\textsf{iA}(R_{2})
and thus
\alpha
cannot be an initial action

R_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{}\,$}R_{2}^{\prime}
,
by Lem.
C.44
(2). Now we have
\{\alpha,\sigma\}\cap\mathcal{C}\neq\{\,\}
. But then the coherence Def.
12
(2) implies that the c-actions
\alpha{:}H_{1}[R_{1}]
and
\sigma{:}H_{2}[R_{2}]
must interfere.
Since
\alpha\neq\sigma
this implies
\alpha\in H_{2}
or
\sigma\in H_{1}
, or
\alpha=\tau
and
H_{2}\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{2})\cup\{\tau\})\neq\{\,\}
. Since
R_{2}={{\mathsf{0}}}
the latter reduces to
\alpha=\tau\in H_{2}
.
The last part of the Proposition now follows from the fact that the clock transition is enabled and thus
\tau\not\in H_{2}
.

Proof D.63

(Of Thm.
14
)
.

Let
P
be coherent and
Q
a derivative with c-enabled reductions

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}Q_{1}\text{ and }

Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}Q_{2}.
If
Q_{1}\equiv Q_{2}
we are done immediately. Suppose
Q_{1}\not\equiv Q_{2}
. Then c-enabling implies
\tau\not\in H_{i}
as well as
H_{i}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{i})=\{\,\}
, which implies
H_{i}\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{i})\cup\{\tau\})=\{\,\}
. But then the c-actions
\tau{:}H_{1}[R_{1}]
and
\tau{:}H_{2}[R_{2}]
are interference-free, whence coherence Def.
12
(2) implies there exist
H_{i}^{\prime}
and processes
R_{i}^{\prime}
,
Q^{\prime}
such that

Q_{1}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{2}^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}\text{ and }

Q_{2}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{1}^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}

\text{and }R_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}R_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }R_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}}R_{2}^{\prime}
with
H_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{i}
.
Finally, observe that
\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{i}^{\prime})\subseteq\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{i})
by Lem.
C.46
from which we infer

H_{i}^{\prime}\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{i}^{\prime})\cup\{\tau\})\subseteq H_{i}\cap(\overline{\textsf{iA}}^{{}_{\ast}}(R_{i})\cup\{\tau\}).
Thus, the reconverging reductions

Q_{i}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R_{3-i}^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\tau}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{3-i}^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}

are again c-enabled. This was to be shown.

Proof D.64

(Of Prop.
15
)
.

The first part of the statement follows directly from Def.
12
(2): The c-actions
\ell{:}H_{i}[R_{i}]
are trivially interference-free. Now if
P_{1}\nequiv P_{2}
, then coherence gives us a strong environment shifts implying
\ell\in\textsf{iA}(R_{1})
.
The second part is because by Lem.
C.44
(1) we have
R_{i}={{\mathsf{0}}}
and thus
\textsf{iA}(R_{i})=\{\,\}
when
\ell\in C
.

Lemma D.65
.

Let
P_{1}\Vdash\pi
and
P_{2}\Vdash\pi
be coherent for the same pivot policy
\pi
. If
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are single-threaded, then
every admissible transition of
P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}
is c-enabled.

Proof D.66
.

Consider an admissible transition of
P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}
where
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are single-threaded. Firstly, by Lem.
C.44
, each of the single-threaded processes can only generate c-actions
\ell_{i}{:}H_{i}[R_{i}]
where
\tau\not\in H_{i}
and
R_{i}={{\mathsf{0}}}
. Such transitions are always c-enabled for trivial reasons. Further, any rendez-vous synchronisation of such an
\ell_{1}{:}H_{1}[R_{1}]
and
\ell_{2}{:}H_{2}[R_{2}]
generates a c-action
\tau{:}(H_{1}\cup H_{2}\cup B)[R_{1}\,|\,R_{2}]
with
R_{1}\,|\,R_{2}={{\mathsf{0}}}
.
Because of Lem.
C.56
, the set

B=\{\tau\mid H_{1}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{2})\subseteq\{\ell\}\text{ or }H_{2}\cap\overline{\textsf{iA}}(P_{1})\subseteq\{\overline{\ell}\}\}

must be empty. Thus, the reduction
\ell_{1}\,|\,\ell_{2}
is c-enabled, too.

The following, slightly extended version of Prop.
23
says that a pivotable process cannot offer a clock action and exhibit another clock or a rendez-vous synchronisation at the same time. This means that in this class of processes, clocks and reductions are sequentially scheduled.

Proposition D.67

(Sequential Schedule and Clock Maximal Progress)
.

Suppose
P
is coherent and pivotable and
\sigma\in\textsf{iA}(P)
. Then, for all
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
with
\ell\neq\sigma
we have
\ell\not\in\textsf{iA}(P)
or
\overline{\ell}\not\in\textsf{iA}(P)
. In particular,
P
is in normal form, i.e., there is no reduction
P\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}}P^{\prime}
.

Proof D.68
.

The proof proceeds by contradiction. Let
P\Vdash\pi
for pivot policy
\pi
. Suppose
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
,
\ell\neq\sigma
and

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{{{\mathsf{0}}}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\sigma}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P_{1}\text{ and }P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{F}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{N}}\,$}P_{2}\text{ and }P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{G}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\overline{\ell}}$}}\!\!{{}_{M}}\,$}P_{2}.
By Prop.
D.61
we infer
\sigma\in N
or
\ell\in H
, i.e., by conformance Def.
17
we have
\pi\Vdash\sigma\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell
or
\pi\Vdash\ell\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\sigma
. Hence,
\pi\nVdash\sigma\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell
. For the same reason we have
\pi\nVdash\sigma\mathrel{\diamond}{}\overline{\ell}
.
However, this contradicts the pivot property of
\pi
, which requires
\pi\Vdash\sigma\mathrel{\diamond}{}\ell
or
\pi\Vdash\sigma\mathrel{\diamond}{}\overline{\ell}
. Finally, if
P\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\rightarrow}}}P^{\prime}
then the reduction must arise from a rendez-vous synchronisation inside
P
, i.e., there is
\ell\in\mathcal{R}
with
\ell\in\textsf{iA}(P)
and
\overline{\ell}\in\textsf{iA}(P)
. But then
\sigma\in\textsf{iA}(P)
is impossible as we have just seen.

The proof of Thm.
24
will be conducted in the following Secs.
D.1
–
D.4
. It proceeds by induction on the structure of derivations in the SOS of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{spt}}
, given in Fig.
2
. We first show that the indirections of syntactic transformations via the static laws of Fig.
1
, built into the SOS by the
(\textit{Struct})
rule, can in fact be eliminated in terms of a finite number of standard SOS rules that do not refer to structural laws.

Definition D.69
.

A process
P
is called
{{\mathsf{0}}}
-free if either
P={{\mathsf{0}}}
or all occurrences of
{{\mathsf{0}}}
in
P
are guarded by a prefix
\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}R
.

Proposition D.70
.

Every process
P
is structurally congruent to a
{{\mathsf{0}}}
-free process
Q
.

Proof D.71
.

By application of the laws

P\star{{\mathsf{0}}}\equiv P
and
{{\mathsf{0}}}\wr L\equiv{{\mathsf{0}}}
, where
\star\in\{\,|\,,+\}
and
\wr\in\{\,\backslash\,,\,/\penalty\,\}
we can eliminate every occurrence of
{{\mathsf{0}}}
in an process
P
that is not of the form
\alpha{:}H{\mathsf{.}}{{\mathsf{0}}}
.

Proposition D.72
.

If
P
is a
{{\mathsf{0}}}
-free process, and there is a derivation
{\cal D}
for
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q
, then all uses of the rule
(\textit{Struct})
in
{\cal D}
can be eliminated in terms of the following symmetric versions of the rules
(\textit{Par})
and
(\textit{Sum})
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\qquad}r}P+Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}&P+Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}\\[14.22636pt]
P\,|\,Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,|\,Q}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\,|\,Q\lx@proof@logical@and P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}\alpha\not\in\mathcal{C}&P\,|\,Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R\,|\,Q}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P\,|\,Q^{\prime}\lx@proof@logical@and Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}\alpha\not\in\mathcal{C}\end{array}

More specifically, there is a derivation
{\cal D^{\prime}}
for
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}
with
R^{\prime}\equiv R
and
Q^{\prime}\equiv Q
.

Proof D.73

(Proof Sketch)
.

By induction on the structure of processes one shows that the transitions generated by the SOS with
(\textit{Struct})
can be
simulated by the extended SOS without
(\textit{Struct})
. The argument is based on the following case analysis:

•

If
P={{\mathsf{0}}}
then no process structurally equivalent to
P
can generate any transition: If
P\equiv Q
and
Q\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}
then
P\neq{{\mathsf{0}}}
. Hence, the statement of the proposition holds trivially.

•

For prefixes we show that if
P\equiv\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
and
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell^{\prime}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}
then
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
,
H^{\prime}=H
,
E^{\prime}\equiv{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
Q^{\prime}\equiv Q
.

•

For sums we observe that if
P\equiv P_{1}+P_{2}
and
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q
then there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
P_{i}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\ell}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}Q^{\prime}
such that
E^{\prime}\equiv E
and
Q^{\prime}\equiv Q
.

•

For all other operators, e.g.
P\equiv P_{1}\,|\,P_{2}
,
P\equiv Q\,/\penalty\,L
,
P\equiv Q\,\backslash\,L
, we can easily show that transitions
P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{R}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha}$}}\!\!{{}_{H}}\,$}P^{\prime}
can be obtained from transitions of
P_{i}
and
Q
.

Note that the restriction in Prop.
D.72
is necessary. If
P
is not
{{\mathsf{0}}}
-free, say
P={{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,Q
, then we can have
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,Q\xrightarrow{\sigma}Q^{\prime}
because of
(\textit{Struct})
, because
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,Q\equiv Q
and
Q\xrightarrow{\sigma}Q^{\prime}
, but without
(\textit{Struct})
we could not derive
{{\mathsf{0}}}\,|\,Q\xrightarrow{\sigma}Q^{\prime}
.

Because of Prop.
D.70
we may assume our processes are
{{\mathsf{0}}}
-free.
Because of Prop.
D.72
we may prove the preservation of coherence (for
{{\mathsf{0}}}
-free processes) by induction on the SOS rules without considering
(\textit{Struct})
, but including the symmetric rules
(\textit{Par}_{i})
and
(\textit{Sum}_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

D.1
Stop and Prefixes

Proposition D.74
.

The process
{{\mathsf{0}}}
is coherent for any policy, i.e.,
{{\mathsf{0}}}\Vdash\pi
for all
\pi
.

Proof D.75
.

The inactive process
{{\mathsf{0}}}
is locally coherent for trivial reasons, simply because it does not offer any transitions at all. There is no SOS rule applicable to it in the
(\textit{Struct})
-free setting. Note that in the system with
(\textit{Struct})
rule, we would have to argue that no process structurally equivalent to
{{\mathsf{0}}}
has any transitions.

Proposition D.76
.

Let process
Q
be coherent for
\pi
. Then, for every action
\ell\in\mathcal{R}
the prefix expression
\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
is coherent for
\pi
, if
\ell\in H\subseteq\{\ell^{\prime}\mid\ell^{\prime}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\ell\in\pi\}
.

Proof D.77
.

A prefix expression
P=\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
generates only a single transition by rule
(\textit{Act})
, so that the assumption

P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{1}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{1}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{1}}}\,$}Q_{1}\text{ and }P\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E_{2}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha_{2}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H_{2}}}\,$}Q_{2}

(1)

implies
\alpha_{i}=\ell
,
H_{i}=H
,
E_{i}\equiv{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
Q_{i}\equiv Q
.
It is easy to see that
\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
conforms to
\pi
if
H\subseteq\{\beta\mid\beta\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\;\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}{}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{3.24445pt}{\rule{2.0pt}{0.4pt}}\hskip-6.75003pt\raisebox{6.22778pt}{\hskip 3.00003pt\scriptsize$$\hskip 3.00003pt}\hskip 0.74997pt\raisebox{0.22223pt}{\scalebox{.5}[.5]{$\succ$}}\;$}\alpha\in\pi\}
.
Since
\alpha_{i}=\ell\in H=H_{i}
and
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}\subseteq\mathcal{R}

nothing needs to be proved.
Finally, note that the only immediate derivative of
\alpha{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q
is
Q
which is coherent for
\pi
by assumption.

Again, it is important to notice that the SOS with
(\textit{Struct})
would not immediately warrant the conclusion that both derivations (
1
) are by
(\textit{Act})
. This is because these transitions could be the transitions of two syntactically distinct (but structurally congruent) expressions
P_{1}\equiv P\equiv P_{2}
. Instead, we would have to argue that if
P=\ell{:}H{\mathsf{.}}Q\equiv P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\mbox{ $\xrightarrow[\raisebox{-2.11113pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{E^{\prime}}$}]{\raisebox{-4.22227pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{${}^{\alpha^{\prime}}$}}\!\!{{}_{H^{\prime}}}\,$}Q^{\prime}
then
\alpha^{\prime}=\ell
,
H^{\prime}=H
,
E^{\prime}\equiv{{\mathsf{0}}}
and
Q^{\prime}\equiv Q
